https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing 0


<dt id=""absorbency""><dfn>Absorbency</dfn></dt>
<dd>Absorbency is a measure of how much amount of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water"" title=""Water"">water</a> a fabric can <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absorption_(chemistry)"" title=""Absorption (chemistry)"">absorb</a> as well as how fast it can absorb it.<sup about=""#mwt19"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-3"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-3""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-3"" id=""mwIg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwIw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwJA"">[</span>3<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwJQ"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""acetate""><dfn>Acetate</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellulose_acetate"" title=""Cellulose acetate"">Acetate</a> is a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic_fiber"" title=""Synthetic fiber"">synthetic fiber</a> created from wood pulp cellulose. It is a cheaper alternative to satin and charmeuse because it is soft, luxurious, wrinkle-resistant, and drapes well. However, the fabric does melt easily.<sup about=""#mwt26"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-4"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-4""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-4"" id=""mwJg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwJw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwKA"">[</span>4<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwKQ"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""acrylic""><dfn>Acrylic</dfn></dt><dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acrylic_fiber"" title=""Acrylic fiber"">Acrylic fiber</a> is a synthetic <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polymer"" title=""Polymer"">polymer</a> fiber that contains at least 85% <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acrylonitrile"" title=""Acrylonitrile"">acrylonitrile</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""aida_cloth""><dfn>Aida cloth</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aida_cloth"" title=""Aida cloth"">Aida cloth</a> is a coarse open-weave fabric traditionally used for <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-stitch"" title=""Cross-stitch"">cross-stitch</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""alnage""><dfn>Alnage</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alnage"" title=""Alnage"">Alnage</a> is the official supervision of the shape and quality of manufactured woolen cloth.</dd>

<dt id=""alpaca""><dfn>Alpaca</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpaca"" title=""Alpaca"">Alpaca</a> is a name given to two distinct things:
<ul><li>The wool of the Peruvian alpaca.</li>
<li>A style of fabric originally made from alpaca fiber but now frequently made from a similar type of fiber.</li></ul></dd>

<dt id=""angora""><dfn>Angora</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angora_wool"" title=""Angora wool"">Angora</a> refers to the hair of the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angora_rabbit"" title=""Angora rabbit"">angora rabbit</a>, or the fabric made from angora rabbit fur. (Fabric made from <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angora_goat"" title=""Angora goat"">angora goat</a> is mohair.)<sup about=""#mwt40"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-5"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-5""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-5"" id=""mwKg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwKw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwLA"">[</span>5<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwLQ"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""appliqué""><dfn>Appliqué</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appliqué"" title=""Appliqué"">Appliqué</a> is a sewing technique in which fabric shapes, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lace"" title=""Lace"">lace</a> or trim, are sewn onto a foundation fabric to create designs.<sup about=""#mwt44"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPicken19578_6-0"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{""group"":"""",""name"":""FOOTNOTEPicken19578""},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPicken19578-6""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPicken19578-6"" id=""mwLg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwLw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwMA"">[</span>6<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwMQ"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""aramid""><dfn>Aramid</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramid"" title=""Aramid"">Aramid</a> fiber is a fire-resistant and strong synthetic fiber.</dd>

<dt id=""argyle""><dfn>Argyle</dfn></dt>
<dd>An <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argyle_(pattern)"" title=""Argyle (pattern)"">argyle</a> pattern is one containing diamonds in a sort of diagonal checkerboard pattern.</dd>

</dl>

Often of silk or silk blended with wool, used for neckties, women&apos;s fine suits and coats, men&apos;s and women&apos;s evening wear.""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Batik""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Batik]] is an Indonesian traditional word and refers to a generic [[wax]]-resist dyeing technique used on fabric.""}},""i"":12}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Bedford cord""}},""i"":13}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Bedford cord]] is a combination of two kinds of weave, namely plain and drill. It is a durable fabric that is often used in upholstery or outerwear.""}},""i"":14}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Bias""}},""i"":15}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The [[Bias (textile)|bias]] direction of a piece of woven fabric, usually referred to simply as \""&apos;&apos;&apos;the bias&apos;&apos;&apos;\"", is at 45 degrees to its warp and weft threads.  Every piece of woven fabric has two biases, [[perpendicular]] to each other.""}},""i"":16}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Binding""}},""i"":17}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""In sewing, [[Binding (sewing)|binding]] is used as both a noun and a verb to refer to &apos;&apos;finishing&apos;&apos; a seam or hem of a garment, usually by rolling or pressing then stitching on an edging or trim.""}},""i"":18}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Blend""}},""i"":19}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[Blend (textile)|blend]] is yarn obtained when two or more staple fibers are combined in a textile process for producing spun yarns.""}},""i"":20}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Bobbin""}},""i"":21}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[bobbin]] is a spindle or cylinder, with or without [[flange]]s, on which [[wire]], [[yarn]], thread or [[Roll film|film]] is wound.""}},""i"":22}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Bobbin lace""}},""i"":23}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Bobbin lace]] is a delicate lace that uses wound spools of thread (the [[bobbin]]s) to weave together the shapes in the lace.""}},""i"":24}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Bobbinet""}},""i"":25}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Bobbinet]] is a tulle netting with hexagonal shaped holes, traditionally used as a base for embroidery and lingerie.""}},""i"":26}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Bolt""}},""i"":27}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A bolt is a standard commercial textile unit comprising a length of fabric rolled around a flat or tube. They come in widths ranging from 35-60 inches, while length varies based on type of material.&lt;ref>{{cite book|title=Sewing for Dummies|last=Maresh|first=Jan Saunders|isbn=9780470887585|date=2010|publisher=Wiley}}&lt;/ref>""}},""i"":28}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Bombazine""}},""i"":29}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Bombazine]] is a fabric originally made of silk or silk and wool, and now also made of cotton and wool or of wool alone. It is twilled or corded and used for dress-material.""}},""i"":30}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Braid""}},""i"":31}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""To [[braid]] is to interweave or twine three or more separate strands of one or more materials in a diagonally overlapping pattern.""}},""i"":32}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Broadcloth""}},""i"":33}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Broadcloth]] is a material of superior quality.{{citation needed|date=May 2025}} It is a densely-woven medium weight cotton with a similar character to poplin. It is semisoft and easy to use.&lt;ref>{{Citation |last=Willard |first=Dana |year=2012 |title=Fabrics A to Z: The Essential Guide to Choosing and Using Fabric for Sewing |publisher=Stewart, Tabori &amp; Chang |publication-place=New York, NY |page=16}}&lt;/ref>""}},""i"":34}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Brocade""}},""i"":35}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Brocade]] is a fabric where the [[pattern]]s are woven with a supplementary weft.""}},""i"":36}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Broella""}},""i"":37}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Broella, from Old French:{{Lang|fr|brouelle}},  is a type of coarse fabric that was commonly used for the everyday attire of both peasants and the monastic clergy during the Middle Ages.&lt;ref>{{Cite book |url=https://books.google.com/books?id=9h5bAAAAYAAJ&amp;dq=%22Broella%22+-wikipedia&amp;pg=PA131 |title=Bulletin |date=1893 |pages=131 |language=en}}&lt;/ref>&lt;ref>{{Cite book |last=Fairholt |first=Frederick William |url=https://books.google.com/books?id=scwDAAAAYAAJ&amp;dq=%22Broella%22+-wikipedia&amp;pg=PA93 |title=Costume in England: A History of Dress to the End of the Eighteenth Century |date=1885 |publisher=G. W. |last3=Cunnington |first3=P. E. |date=2010-11-15 |publisher=Berg |isbn=978-1-84788-533-3 |pages=235 |language=en}}&lt;/ref>""}},""i"":38}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Buckram""}},""i"":39}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Buckram]] is a stiff cloth, made of cotton or linen, which is used to cover, and protect, a [[book]], and although more expensive than its lookalike, Brella, is stronger and resistant to cockroaches eating it. Buckram can also be used to stiffen [[clothes]].""}},""i"":40}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Burlap""}},""i"":41}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Burlap]] is a North American term for a type of cloth often used for sacks. In the UK the equivalent nomenclature is Hessian.""}},""i"":42}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":43}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt53"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt53"" id=""mwNg"">

<dt id=""backstrap_loom""><dfn>Backstrap loom</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Back_strap_loom"" title=""Back strap loom"" class=""mw-redirect"">Backstrap looms</a>, as the name implies, are tied around the weaver's waist on one end and around a stationary object such as a tree, post, or door on the other. Tension can be adjusted simply by leaning back. Backstrap looms are very portable, since they can simply be rolled up and carried.</dd>

<dt id=""baize""><dfn>Baize</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baize"" title=""Baize"">Baize</a> is a coarse <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woollen"" title=""Woollen"" class=""mw-redirect"">woollen</a> or cotton cloth, often coloured red or green.</dd>

<dt id=""ballistic_nylon""><dfn>Ballistic nylon</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballistic_nylon"" title=""Ballistic nylon"">Ballistic nylon</a> is a thick, tough synthetic fabric used for a variety of applications.</dd>

<dt id=""balloon""><dfn>Balloon</dfn></dt>
<dd> The <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balloon_(textile)?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Balloon (textile) (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Balloon (textile)""]}}'>Balloon (textile)</a> is an oscillation pattern of the yarn while unwinding from the bobbin due to centrifugal forces.</dd>

<dt id=""barathea""><dfn>Barathea</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barathea"" title=""Barathea"">Barathea</a> is an indistinct twill or broken rib<span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><span typeof=""mw:Entity"">–</span><span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span>usually a twilled hopsack weave<span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><span typeof=""mw:Entity"">–</span><span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span>with a fine textured, slightly pebbled surface. Often of silk or silk blended with wool, used for neckties, women's fine suits and coats, men's and women's evening wear.</dd>

<dt id=""batik""><dfn>Batik</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batik"" title=""Batik"">Batik</a> is an Indonesian traditional word and refers to a generic <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wax"" title=""Wax"">wax</a>-resist dyeing technique used on fabric.</dd>

<dt id=""bedford_cord""><dfn>Bedford cord</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedford_cord"" title=""Bedford cord"">Bedford cord</a> is a combination of two kinds of weave, namely plain and drill. It is a durable fabric that is often used in upholstery or outerwear.</dd>

<dt id=""bias""><dfn>Bias</dfn></dt>
<dd>The <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bias_(textile)"" title=""Bias (textile)"" class=""mw-redirect"">bias</a> direction of a piece of woven fabric, usually referred to simply as ""<b>the bias</b>"", is at 45 degrees to its warp and weft threads.  Every piece of woven fabric has two biases, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perpendicular"" title=""Perpendicular"">perpendicular</a> to each other.</dd>

<dt id=""binding""><dfn>Binding</dfn></dt>
<dd>In sewing, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binding_(sewing)"" title=""Binding (sewing)"">binding</a> is used as both a noun and a verb to refer to <i>finishing</i> a seam or hem of a garment, usually by rolling or pressing then stitching on an edging or trim.</dd>

<dt id=""blend""><dfn>Blend</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blend_(textile)"" title=""Blend (textile)"">blend</a> is yarn obtained when two or more staple fibers are combined in a textile process for producing spun yarns.</dd>

<dt id=""bobbin""><dfn>Bobbin</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobbin"" title=""Bobbin"">bobbin</a> is a spindle or cylinder, with or without <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flange"" title=""Flange"">flanges</a>, on which <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wire"" title=""Wire"">wire</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarn"" title=""Yarn"">yarn</a>, thread or <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roll_film"" title=""Roll film"">film</a> is wound.</dd>

<dt id=""bobbin_lace""><dfn>Bobbin lace</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobbin_lace"" title=""Bobbin lace"">Bobbin lace</a> is a delicate lace that uses wound spools of thread (the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobbin"" title=""Bobbin"">bobbins</a>) to weave together the shapes in the lace.</dd>

<dt id=""bobbinet""><dfn>Bobbinet</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobbinet"" title=""Bobbinet"">Bobbinet</a> is a tulle netting with hexagonal shaped holes, traditionally used as a base for embroidery and lingerie.</dd>

<dt id=""bolt""><dfn>Bolt</dfn></dt>
<dd>A bolt is a standard commercial textile unit comprising a length of fabric rolled around a flat or tube. They come in widths ranging from 35-60 inches, while length varies based on type of material.<sup about=""#mwt85"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-7"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-7""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-7"" id=""mwNw""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwOA""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwOQ"">[</span>7<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwOg"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""bombazine""><dfn>Bombazine</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombazine"" title=""Bombazine"">Bombazine</a> is a fabric originally made of silk or silk and wool, and now also made of cotton and wool or of wool alone. It is twilled or corded and used for dress-material.</dd>

<dt id=""braid""><dfn>Braid</dfn></dt>
<dd>To <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braid"" title=""Braid"">braid</a> is to interweave or twine three or more separate strands of one or more materials in a diagonally overlapping pattern.</dd>

<dt id=""broadcloth""><dfn>Broadcloth</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcloth"" title=""Broadcloth"">Broadcloth</a> is a material of superior quality.<link rel=""mw:PageProp/Category"" href=""./Category:All_articles_with_unsourced_statements""/><link rel=""mw:PageProp/Category"" href=""./Category:Articles_with_unsourced_statements_from_May_2025""/><sup class=""noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact"" style=""white-space:nowrap;""><span typeof=""mw:Entity"">[</span><i><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed"" title=""Wikipedia:Citation needed""><span title=""This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2025)"">citation needed</span></a></i><span typeof=""mw:Entity"">]</span></sup> It is a densely-woven medium weight cotton with a similar character to poplin. It is semisoft and easy to use.<sup about=""#mwt96"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-8"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-8""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-8"" id=""mwOw""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwPA""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwPQ"">[</span>8<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwPg"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""brocade""><dfn>Brocade</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brocade"" title=""Brocade"">Brocade</a> is a fabric where the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pattern"" title=""Pattern"">patterns</a> are woven with a supplementary weft.</dd>

<dt id=""broella""><dfn>Broella</dfn></dt>
<dd>Broella, from Old French:<span title=""French-language text""><i lang=""fr"">brouelle</i></span><link rel=""mw:PageProp/Category"" href=""./Category:Articles_containing_French-language_text""/>,  is a type of coarse fabric that was commonly used for the everyday attire of both peasants and the monastic clergy during the Middle Ages.<sup about=""#mwt105"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-9"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-9""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-9"" id=""mwPw""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwQA""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwQQ"">[</span>9<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwQg"">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about=""#mwt110"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-10"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-10""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-10"" id=""mwQw""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwRA""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwRQ"">[</span>10<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwRg"">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about=""#mwt115"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-11"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-11""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-11"" id=""mwRw""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwSA""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwSQ"">[</span>11<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwSg"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""buckram""><dfn>Buckram</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buckram"" title=""Buckram"">Buckram</a> is a stiff cloth, made of cotton or linen, which is used to cover, and protect, a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book"" title=""Book"">book</a>, and although more expensive than its lookalike, Brella, is stronger and resistant to cockroaches eating it. Buckram can also be used to stiffen <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothes"" title=""Clothes"" class=""mw-redirect"">clothes</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""burlap""><dfn>Burlap</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burlap"" title=""Burlap"" class=""mw-redirect"">Burlap</a> is a North American term for a type of cloth often used for sacks. In the UK the equivalent nomenclature is Hessian.</dd>

</dl>

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""3"" id=""mwTQ"" aria-labelledby=""C""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""C"">C</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3"" title=""Edit section: C""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt125"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwTg"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Calico""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Calico (fabric)|Calico]] is a type of fabric made from unbleached, and often not fully processed, cotton. Camel hair can produce a variety of different coarseness of yarn. This fiber is a novelty fiber spun by hand-spinners.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Canvas""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Canvas]] is an extremely heavy-duty fabric used for making [[sail]]s, [[tent]]s, [[Tent#Marquees and larger tents|marquees]], and other functions where sturdiness is required. It is also popularly used on fashion handbags.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Canvas work""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Canvas work]] is embroidery on canvas.""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Carding""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Carding]] is the process of removing dirt and matter and then separating the fibers and roughly aligning them so they all lie in the same direction.""}},""i"":12}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Carpet""}},""i"":13}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[carpet]] is any loom-woven, felted textile or grass floor covering.""}},""i"":14}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Cashmere""}},""i"":15}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Cashmere wool|Cashmere]] is wool from the Cashmere [[goat]].""}},""i"":16}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Cellulose""}},""i"":17}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Cellulose]] fiber can be processed to make [[cellophane]] and rayon, and more recently Modal.""}},""i"":18}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Cheesecloth""}},""i"":19}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Cheesecloth]] is a loosewoven cotton cloth, such as is used in pressing [[cheese]] curds.""}},""i"":20}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Chiffon""}},""i"":21}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Chiffon (fabric)|Chiffon]] is a sheer fabric made of silk or rayon.""}},""i"":22}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Chino cloth""}},""i"":23}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Chino cloth]] is a kind of twill fabric, usually made primarily from cotton.""}},""i"":24}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Chintz""}},""i"":25}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Chintz]] is calico cloth printed with flowers and other devices in different colors. It was originally of Eastern manufacture.""}},""i"":26}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Coir""}},""i"":27}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Coir]] is a coarse fiber extracted from the fibrous outer shell of a [[coconut]].""}},""i"":28}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Colorfast (colourfast)""}},""i"":29}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A textile&apos;s ability to [[Colour fastness|maintain its color]] without running or fading.""}},""i"":30}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Cord""}},""i"":31}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Cord is twisted fiber, usually intermediate between [[rope]] and string.  It is also used as a shortened form of corduroy.""}},""i"":32}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""corduroy""}},""i"":33}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Corduroy]] is a durable cloth.""}},""i"":34}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""cotton""}},""i"":35}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Cotton]] is a soft fiber that grows around the seeds of the [[cotton plant]], a [[shrub]] native to the tropical and subtropical regions of both the [[Old World]] and the [[New World]].  The fiber is most often spun into thread and used to make a soft, breathable textile.""}},""i"":36}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""cotton duck""}},""i"":37}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Cotton duck]], sometimes known as duck cloth, or simply duck, is a heavy, plain woven cotton fabric similar to canvas. It is more tightly woven than canvas and is used in a variety of applications where durability and a certain degree of water resistance is preferred.""}},""i"":38}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""crash""}},""i"":39}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Crash (fabric)|Crash]] is a rough fabric made from yarns that are usually undyed. The coarsest type is called Russian crash. Linen is generally used for the warp yarn, while linen and jute are used for the filler.""}},""i"":40}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""crepe""}},""i"":41}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Crape|Crepe]] is a silk fabric of a gauzy texture, having a peculiar crisp or crimpy appearance.""}},""i"":42}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""crazy quilt""}},""i"":43}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Crazy quilting]] is the [[textile arts|textile art]] of patchworking.""}},""i"":44}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""crinoline""}},""i"":45}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Crinoline]] was originally a stiff fabric with a weft of [[horse|horse-hair]] and a warp of cotton or linen thread. The fabric first appeared around 1830.""}},""i"":46}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Cross-stitch""}},""i"":47}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Cross-stitch]] is a popular form of [[counted-thread embroidery]] in which X-shaped stitches are used to form a picture.""}},""i"":48}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Crochet""}},""i"":49}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Crochet]] is the process of creating fabric from a length of cord, yarn, or thread with a hooked tool.""}},""i"":50}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Crochet hook""}},""i"":51}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[crochet hook]] is a type of needle, usually with a hook at one end, used to draw thread through knotted loops.""}},""i"":52}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Cro-hook""}},""i"":53}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The [[cro-hook]] is a special double-ended crochet hook used to make double-sided crochet. Because the hook has two ends, two colours of thread can be handled at once and freely interchanged.""}},""i"":54}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":55}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt125"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt125"" id=""mwTw"">

<dt id=""calico""><dfn>Calico</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calico_(fabric)"" title=""Calico (fabric)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Calico</a> is a type of fabric made from unbleached, and often not fully processed, cotton. Also refers to a type of printing.</dd>

<dt id=""cambric""><dfn>Cambric</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambric"" title=""Cambric"">Cambric</a> is a lightweight cotton cloth used as fabric for lace and needlework.</dd>

<dt id=""camel's_hair""><dfn>Camel's hair</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camel"" title=""Camel"">Camel</a>'s hair is a natural fiber from the camel. Camel hair can produce a variety of different coarseness of yarn. This fiber is a novelty fiber spun by hand-spinners.</dd>

<dt id=""canvas""><dfn>Canvas</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canvas"" title=""Canvas"">Canvas</a> is an extremely heavy-duty fabric used for making <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sail"" title=""Sail"">sails</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tent"" title=""Tent"">tents</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tent#Marquees_and_larger_tents"" title=""Tent"">marquees</a>, and other functions where sturdiness is required. It is also popularly used on fashion handbags.</dd>

<dt id=""canvas_work""><dfn>Canvas work</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canvas_work"" title=""Canvas work"" class=""mw-redirect"">Canvas work</a> is embroidery on canvas.</dd>

<dt id=""carding""><dfn>Carding</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carding"" title=""Carding"">Carding</a> is the process of removing dirt and matter and then separating the fibers and roughly aligning them so they all lie in the same direction.</dd>

<dt id=""carpet""><dfn>Carpet</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpet"" title=""Carpet"">carpet</a> is any loom-woven, felted textile or grass floor covering.</dd>

<dt id=""cashmere""><dfn>Cashmere</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cashmere_wool"" title=""Cashmere wool"">Cashmere</a> is wool from the Cashmere <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goat"" title=""Goat"">goat</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""cellulose""><dfn>Cellulose</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellulose"" title=""Cellulose"">Cellulose</a> fiber can be processed to make <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellophane"" title=""Cellophane"">cellophane</a> and rayon, and more recently Modal.</dd>

<dt id=""cheesecloth""><dfn>Cheesecloth</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheesecloth"" title=""Cheesecloth"">Cheesecloth</a> is a loosewoven cotton cloth, such as is used in pressing <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheese"" title=""Cheese"">cheese</a> curds.</dd>

<dt id=""chiffon""><dfn>Chiffon</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiffon_(fabric)"" title=""Chiffon (fabric)"">Chiffon</a> is a sheer fabric made of silk or rayon.</dd>

<dt id=""chino_cloth""><dfn>Chino cloth</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chino_cloth"" title=""Chino cloth"">Chino cloth</a> is a kind of twill fabric, usually made primarily from cotton.</dd>

<dt id=""chintz""><dfn>Chintz</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chintz"" title=""Chintz"">Chintz</a> is calico cloth printed with flowers and other devices in different colors. It was originally of Eastern manufacture.</dd>

<dt id=""coir""><dfn>Coir</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coir"" title=""Coir"">Coir</a> is a coarse fiber extracted from the fibrous outer shell of a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut"" title=""Coconut"">coconut</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""colorfast_(colourfast)""><dfn>Colorfast (colourfast)</dfn></dt>
<dd>A textile's ability to <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colour_fastness"" title=""Colour fastness"">maintain its color</a> without running or fading.</dd>

<dt id=""cord""><dfn>Cord</dfn></dt>
<dd>Cord is twisted fiber, usually intermediate between <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rope"" title=""Rope"">rope</a> and string.  It is also used as a shortened form of corduroy.</dd>

<dt id=""corduroy""><dfn>corduroy</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corduroy"" title=""Corduroy"">Corduroy</a> is a durable cloth.</dd>

<dt id=""cotton""><dfn>cotton</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton"" title=""Cotton"">Cotton</a> is a soft fiber that grows around the seeds of the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_plant"" title=""Cotton plant"" class=""mw-redirect"">cotton plant</a>, a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrub"" title=""Shrub"">shrub</a> native to the tropical and subtropical regions of both the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World"" title=""Old World"">Old World</a> and the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World"" title=""New World"">New World</a>.  The fiber is most often spun into thread and used to make a soft, breathable textile.</dd>

<dt id=""cotton_duck""><dfn>cotton duck</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_duck"" title=""Cotton duck"">Cotton duck</a>, sometimes known as duck cloth, or simply duck, is a heavy, plain woven cotton fabric similar to canvas. It is more tightly woven than canvas and is used in a variety of applications where durability and a certain degree of water resistance is preferred.</dd>

<dt id=""crash""><dfn>crash</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crash_(fabric)"" title=""Crash (fabric)"">Crash</a> is a rough fabric made from yarns that are usually undyed. The coarsest type is called Russian crash. Linen is generally used for the warp yarn, while linen and jute are used for the filler.</dd>

<dt id=""crepe""><dfn>crepe</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crape"" title=""Crape"" class=""mw-redirect"">Crepe</a> is a silk fabric of a gauzy texture, having a peculiar crisp or crimpy appearance.</dd>

<dt id=""crazy_quilt""><dfn>crazy quilt</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crazy_quilting"" title=""Crazy quilting"">Crazy quilting</a> is the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_arts"" title=""Textile arts"">textile art</a> of patchworking.</dd>

<dt id=""crinoline""><dfn>crinoline</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crinoline"" title=""Crinoline"">Crinoline</a> was originally a stiff fabric with a weft of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse"" title=""Horse"">horse-hair</a> and a warp of cotton or linen thread. The fabric first appeared around 1830.</dd>

<dt id=""cross-stitch""><dfn>Cross-stitch</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-stitch"" title=""Cross-stitch"">Cross-stitch</a> is a popular form of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counted-thread_embroidery"" title=""Counted-thread embroidery"">counted-thread embroidery</a> in which X-shaped stitches are used to form a picture.</dd>

<dt id=""crochet""><dfn>Crochet</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crochet"" title=""Crochet"">Crochet</a> is the process of creating fabric from a length of cord, yarn, or thread with a hooked tool.</dd>

<dt id=""crochet_hook""><dfn>Crochet hook</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crochet_hook"" title=""Crochet hook"">crochet hook</a> is a type of needle, usually with a hook at one end, used to draw thread through knotted loops.</dd>

<dt id=""cro-hook""><dfn>Cro-hook</dfn></dt>
<dd>The <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cro-hook"" title=""Cro-hook"">cro-hook</a> is a special double-ended crochet hook used to make double-sided crochet. Because the hook has two ends, two colours of thread can be handled at once and freely interchanged.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt182"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwUA"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt182"" id=""mwUQ""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt183"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""4"" id=""mwUg"" aria-labelledby=""D""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""D"">D</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4"" title=""Edit section: D""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt184"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwUw"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""damask""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Damask]] is a fabric of silk, wool, linen, cotton, or synthetic fibers, with a pattern formed by weaving. Today, it generally denotes a linen texture richly figured in the weaving with flowers, fruit, forms of animal life, and other types of ornament.""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""darning mushroom""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[darning mushroom]] is a tool which can be used for [[darning]] clothes, particularly [[sock]]s. The sock can be stretched over the top of the (curved) mushroom, and gathered tightly around the stalk.""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""denim""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Denim]] denotes a rugged cotton twill textile.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""dimity""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Dimity]] is a lightweight, sheer cotton fabric having at least two warp threads thrown into relief to form fine cords.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""dobby loom""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[dobby loom]] is a loom in which each [[harness (weaving)|harness]] can be manipulated individually.  This is in contrast to a [[treadle]] loom, where the harnesses are attached to a number of different treadles depending on the weave structure.""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""double weave""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Double weave]] is a type of advanced weave.  It is done by interlacing two or more sets of warps with two or more sets of filling yarns.""}},""i"":12}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""dowlas""}},""i"":13}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Dowlas]] is the name given to a plain cloth, similar to [[Bed sheet|sheeting]], but usually coarser.""}},""i"":14}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""durability""}},""i"":15}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""How [[Durable good|durable]] a fabric or yarn is.""}},""i"":16}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""dyes""}},""i"":17}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Dye]] is used to color fabric. There are two main types, [[natural dye]]s and [[synthetic dye]]s. The process is called [[dyeing]].""}},""i"":18}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""dye lot""}},""i"":19}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The [[dye lot]] is a number that identifies yarns dyed in the same vat at the same time.  Subtle differences can appear between different batches of the same color yarn from the same manufacturer.""}},""i"":20}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":21}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt184"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt184"" id=""mwVA"">

<dt id=""damask""><dfn>damask</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damask"" title=""Damask"">Damask</a> is a fabric of silk, wool, linen, cotton, or synthetic fibers, with a pattern formed by weaving. Today, it generally denotes a linen texture richly figured in the weaving with flowers, fruit, forms of animal life, and other types of ornament.</dd>

<dt id=""darning_mushroom""><dfn>darning mushroom</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darning_mushroom"" title=""Darning mushroom"" class=""mw-redirect"">darning mushroom</a> is a tool which can be used for <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darning"" title=""Darning"">darning</a> clothes, particularly <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sock"" title=""Sock"">socks</a>. The sock can be stretched over the top of the (curved) mushroom, and gathered tightly around the stalk.</dd>

<dt id=""denim""><dfn>denim</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denim"" title=""Denim"">Denim</a> denotes a rugged cotton twill textile.</dd>

<dt id=""dimity""><dfn>dimity</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimity"" title=""Dimity"">Dimity</a> is a lightweight, sheer cotton fabric having at least two warp threads thrown into relief to form fine cords.</dd>

<dt id=""dobby_loom""><dfn>dobby loom</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobby_loom"" title=""Dobby loom"">dobby loom</a> is a loom in which each <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harness_(weaving)?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Harness (weaving) (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Harness (weaving)""]}}'>harness</a> can be manipulated individually.  This is in contrast to a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treadle"" title=""Treadle"">treadle</a> loom, where the harnesses are attached to a number of different treadles depending on the weave structure.</dd>

<dt id=""double_weave""><dfn>double weave</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_weave"" title=""Double weave"" class=""mw-redirect"">Double weave</a> is a type of advanced weave.  It is done by interlacing two or more sets of warps with two or more sets of filling yarns.</dd>

<dt id=""dowlas""><dfn>dowlas</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dowlas"" title=""Dowlas"">Dowlas</a> is the name given to a plain cloth, similar to <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bed_sheet"" title=""Bed sheet"">sheeting</a>, but usually coarser.</dd>

<dt id=""durability""><dfn>durability</dfn></dt>
<dd>How <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durable_good"" title=""Durable good"">durable</a> a fabric or yarn is.</dd>

<dt id=""dyes""><dfn>dyes</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dye"" title=""Dye"">Dye</a> is used to color fabric. There are two main types, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_dye"" title=""Natural dye"">natural dyes</a> and <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic_dye"" title=""Synthetic dye"" class=""mw-redirect"">synthetic dyes</a>. The process is called <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyeing"" title=""Dyeing"">dyeing</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""dye_lot""><dfn>dye lot</dfn></dt>
<dd>The <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dye_lot"" title=""Dye lot"">dye lot</a> is a number that identifies yarns dyed in the same vat at the same time.  Subtle differences can appear between different batches of the same color yarn from the same manufacturer.</dd>

</dl>

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""5"" id=""mwVw"" aria-labelledby=""E""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""E"">E</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5"" title=""Edit section: E""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt209"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwWA"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""eisengarn""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Eisengarn]], meaning \""iron yarn\"" in English, is a light-reflecting, strong, waxed-cotton thread. It is made by soaking cotton threads in a starch, [[paraffin wax]] solution.  The threads are then stretched and polished. The end result of the process is a lustrous,  tear-resistant yarn which is extremely hardwearing.&lt;ref>{{cite web |url=http://www.wuppertal.ihk24.de/servicemarken/branchen_neu/industrie/industrie_im_bs/902782/Textilstandort_Wuppertal.html |title=Industriegeschichte aus dem Bergischen land |work=IHK Wuppertal-Solingen-Remscheid |language=de |access-date=27 November 2016 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20161127215802/http://www.wuppertal.ihk24.de/servicemarken/branchen_neu/industrie/industrie_im_bs/902782/Textilstandort_Wuppertal.html |archive-date=27 November 2016}}&lt;/ref>&lt;ref>[http://digit.wdr.de/entries/5900?index=69&amp;q=eyJ7aH0iOlsiezE2fSIsInsxYX0iXSwie2t9IjpbInsyMn0iXSwiezF9IjpbeyJmaWVsZCI6IiJ9XX0%3D&amp;qt=search &apos;&apos;Eisengarnfabrikation in Barmen&apos;&apos;] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20161128050159/http://digit.wdr.de/entries/5900?index=69&amp;q=eyJ7aH0iOlsiezE2fSIsInsxYX0iXSwie2t9IjpbInsyMn0iXSwiezF9IjpbeyJmaWVsZCI6IiJ9XX0%3D&amp;qt=search |date=2016-11-28 }}. WDR digit project (Video (16 min) in German). Retrieved 27 November 2016.&lt;/ref> Invented in the 19th Century, it was further developed in 1927 by the textile designer [[Margaretha Reichardt]] at the [[Bauhaus]] for use on [[Marcel Breuer]]&apos;s  tubular-steel chairs.&lt;ref>[https://www.bauhaus100.com/the-bauhaus/people/students/margaretha-reichardt/ &apos;&apos;Margaretha Reichardt&apos;&apos;] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20181101015712/https://www.bauhaus100.com/the-bauhaus/people/students/margaretha-reichardt/ |date=2018-11-01 }}. Bauhaus100. Retrieved 27 November 2016.&lt;/ref>&lt;ref>Kreis Weimarer Land/Angermuseum Erfurt (2009). &apos;&apos;Margaretha Reichardt 1907-1984 Textilkunst&apos;&apos;. Erfurt: Kreis Weimarer Land/Angermuseum Erfurt. p. 19.&lt;/ref>""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""elasticity""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Elasticity (fabrics)|Elasticity]]""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""embroidery""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Embroidery]] is an [[ancient]] variety of decorative needlework in which designs and pictures are created by stitching strands of some material on to a layer of another material. See also: [[machine embroidery]].""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""ends per inch (EPI)""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Units of textile measurement#Ends per inch|Ends per inch]] like &apos;&apos;threads per inch&apos;&apos; is a measure of the coarseness or fineness of fabric, displaying the number of (warp) threads per inch of woven fabric.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""epinglé fabric""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A type of velvet fabric woven on a [[wire loom]] or épinglé loom.  The épinglé velvet is notable in that both a [[loop pile]] and a [[cut pile]] can be integrated into the same fabric.  The art of épinglé weaving in Europe originated from Lucca (Italy) and later came to Venice and Genua, which is where the term &apos;&apos;Genua velvet&apos;&apos; comes from.  The technique of épinglé weaving is still used today in the Flemish region of Kortrijk and Waregem.  The fabric finds it application mostly in upholstery, although in medieval times it was used as apparel for princes and kings as well as for bishops, cardinals, and the Pope.""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""epinglé loom""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A kind of weaving machine whereby steel rods are inserted in a top shed which is formed over the bottom shed in which the weft is inserted. The same mechanism also extracts the rods from the fabric.  If the rod carries a cutting blade at the tip the warps that are woven over the rods are cut, creating a cut pile effect.  In case the rod has no blade, then the warp ends from a loop pile.  Alternating cut and loop wires create cut and loop pile in the fabric. They may be used to reinforce the hole, to shield something from the sharp edges of the hole, or both.""}},""i"":16}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":17}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt209"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt209"" id=""mwWQ"">

<dt id=""eisengarn""><dfn>eisengarn</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eisengarn"" title=""Eisengarn"">Eisengarn</a>, meaning ""iron yarn"" in English, is a light-reflecting, strong, waxed-cotton thread. It is made by soaking cotton threads in a starch, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraffin_wax"" title=""Paraffin wax"">paraffin wax</a> solution.  The threads are then stretched and polished. The end result of the process is a lustrous,  tear-resistant yarn which is extremely hardwearing.<sup about=""#mwt215"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-12"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-12""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-12"" id=""mwWg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwWw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwXA"">[</span>12<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwXQ"">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about=""#mwt219"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-13"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-13""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-13"" id=""mwXg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwXw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwYA"">[</span>13<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwYQ"">]</span></span></a></sup> Invented in the 19th Century, it was further developed in 1927 by the textile designer <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaretha_Reichardt"" title=""Margaretha Reichardt"">Margaretha Reichardt</a> at the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauhaus"" title=""Bauhaus"">Bauhaus</a> for use on <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_Breuer"" title=""Marcel Breuer"">Marcel Breuer</a>'s  tubular-steel chairs.<sup about=""#mwt222"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-14"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-14""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-14"" id=""mwYg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwYw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwZA"">[</span>14<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwZQ"">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about=""#mwt224"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-15"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-15""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-15"" id=""mwZg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwZw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwaA"">[</span>15<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwaQ"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""elasticity""><dfn>elasticity</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elasticity_(fabrics)"" title=""Elasticity (fabrics)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Elasticity</a></dd>

<dt id=""embroidery""><dfn>embroidery</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embroidery"" title=""Embroidery"">Embroidery</a> is an <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient"" title=""Ancient"" class=""mw-redirect"">ancient</a> variety of decorative needlework in which designs and pictures are created by stitching strands of some material on to a layer of another material. See also: <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_embroidery"" title=""Machine embroidery"">machine embroidery</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""ends_per_inch_(epi)""><dfn>ends per inch (EPI)</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Units_of_textile_measurement#Ends_per_inch"" title=""Units of textile measurement"">Ends per inch</a> like <i>threads per inch</i> is a measure of the coarseness or fineness of fabric, displaying the number of (warp) threads per inch of woven fabric.</dd>

<dt id=""epinglé_fabric""><dfn>epinglé fabric</dfn></dt>
<dd>A type of velvet fabric woven on a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wire_loom?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Wire loom (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Wire loom""]}}'>wire loom</a> or épinglé loom.  The épinglé velvet is notable in that both a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loop_pile"" title=""Loop pile"" class=""mw-redirect"">loop pile</a> and a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cut_pile"" title=""Cut pile"" class=""mw-redirect"">cut pile</a> can be integrated into the same fabric.  The art of épinglé weaving in Europe originated from Lucca (Italy) and later came to Venice and Genua, which is where the term <i>Genua velvet</i> comes from.  The technique of épinglé weaving is still used today in the Flemish region of Kortrijk and Waregem.  The fabric finds it application mostly in upholstery, although in medieval times it was used as apparel for princes and kings as well as for bishops, cardinals, and the Pope.</dd>

<dt id=""epinglé_loom""><dfn>epinglé loom</dfn></dt>
<dd>A kind of weaving machine whereby steel rods are inserted in a top shed which is formed over the bottom shed in which the weft is inserted.  The steel rods are inserted into the fabric every second or third pick by a separate mechanism that is synchronised with the weaving motion. The same mechanism also extracts the rods from the fabric.  If the rod carries a cutting blade at the tip the warps that are woven over the rods are cut, creating a cut pile effect.  In case the rod has no blade, then the warp ends from a loop pile.  Alternating cut and loop wires create cut and loop pile in the fabric.  This weaving technology is used for weaving velvets for furnishing and apparel applications.  These fabrics are known as 'moquette' or ""épinglé' fabrics.  This kind of weaving machine is also used for weaving carpets where it is known as a 'Wilton loom'.</dd>

<dt id=""even-weave""><dfn>even-weave</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Even-weave"" title=""Even-weave"" class=""mw-redirect"">Even-weave or evenweave</a> fabric is used in counted-thread embroidery and is characterized by Warp and weft threads of the same size.</dd>

<dt id=""eyelet""><dfn>eyelet</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grommet"" title=""Grommet"">Grommets</a> and <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cutwork"" title=""Cutwork"">eyelets</a> are <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metal"" title=""Metal"">metal</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plastic"" title=""Plastic"">plastic</a>, or <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubber"" title=""Rubber"" class=""mw-redirect"">rubber</a> rings that are inserted into a hole made through another material. They may be used to reinforce the hole, to shield something from the sharp edges of the hole, or both.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt241"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwag"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt241"" id=""mwaw""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt242"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#B"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">B</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#C"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">C</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#D"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">D</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#E"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">E</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#F"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">F</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#G"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">G</a></li>

<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#K"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">K</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#L"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">L</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#M"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">M</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#N"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">N</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#O"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">O</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#P"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">P</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#Q"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">Q</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#R"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">R</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#S"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">S</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#T"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">T</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#U"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">U</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#V"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">V</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#W"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">W</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#X"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">X</a></li>

</div></div></div></div>

felt.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""fil coupé""},""content"":{""wt"":""{{lang|fr|fil coupé}}""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[File:FIL COUPE&apos; MDT TL.CM.22.403 a.jpg|thumb|upright=.5|Round &apos;&apos;fil coupé&apos;&apos; elements on a sheer fabric]] &apos;&apos;Fil coupé&apos;&apos;, from the French \""cut thread\"", is a technique in which an additional set of weft yarns is added to create floating decorative elements on the textile’s surface, the threads of which are cut after weaving.&lt;ref>{{Cite book |last1=Ojala |first1=Aino Elina |last2=Pouta |first2=Emmi Weft""}},""i"":14}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""finishing""}},""i"":15}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Finishing Usually dyed in a dark shade. Declined in popularity from 1813, being replaced by harder wearing and better quality wool cloths.""}},""i"":28}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":29}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt243"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt243"" id=""mwbg"">

<dt id=""facing""><dfn>facing</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facing_(sewing)"" title=""Facing (sewing)"">Facing</a></dd>

<dt id=""felt""><dfn>felt</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felt"" title=""Felt"">Felt</a> is a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-woven"" title=""Non-woven"" class=""mw-redirect"">non-woven</a> cloth that is produced by matting, condensing and pressing fibers. The fibers form the structure of the fabric.</dd>

<dt id=""felting""><dfn>felting</dfn></dt>
<dd>The process of making felt is called <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felting"" title=""Felting"" class=""mw-redirect"">felting</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""fiber""><dfn>fiber</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiber"" title=""Fiber"">Fiber</a> is a class of materials that are continuous filaments or are in discrete elongated pieces, similar to pieces of thread. Fibers are often used in the manufacture of other materials. They can be spun into filaments, thread, or rope. They can be used as a component of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Composite_material"" title=""Composite material"">composite</a> materials. They can also be matted into sheets to make products such as <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper"" title=""Paper"">paper</a> or felt.</dd>

<dt id=""fil_coupé""><dfn><span title=""French-language text""><i lang=""fr"">fil coupé</i></span><link rel=""mw:PageProp/Category"" href=""./Category:Articles_containing_French-language_text""/></dfn></dt>
<dd><figure class=""mw-default-size"" typeof=""mw:File/Thumb""><a href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:FIL_COUPE'_MDT_TL.CM.22.403_a.jpg"" class=""mw-file-description""><img resource=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:FIL_COUPE'_MDT_TL.CM.22.403_a.jpg"" src=""//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/FIL_COUPE%27_MDT_TL.CM.22.403_a.jpg/250px-FIL_COUPE%27_MDT_TL.CM.22.403_a.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail"" decoding=""async"" loading=""lazy"" srcset=""//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/FIL_COUPE%27_MDT_TL.CM.22.403_a.jpg/330px-FIL_COUPE%27_MDT_TL.CM.22.403_a.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x"" data-file-width=""2905"" data-file-height=""3628"" data-file-type=""bitmap"" height=""162"" width=""130"" class=""mw-file-element mw-file-upright"" style=""--mw-file-upright: 0.5""/></a><figcaption>Round <i>fil coupé</i> elements on a sheer fabric</figcaption></figure> <i>Fil coupé</i>, from the French ""cut thread"", is a technique in which an additional set of weft yarns is added to create floating decorative elements on the textile’s surface, the threads of which are cut after weaving.<sup about=""#mwt257"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-16"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-16""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-16"" id=""mwbw""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwcA""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwcQ"">[</span>16<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwcg"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""filament""><dfn>filament</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filament_fiber"" title=""Filament fiber"" class=""mw-redirect"">filament</a> is a fine, thinly spun thread, fiber, or wire.</dd>

<dt id=""filling""><dfn>filling</dfn></dt>
<dd>See Weft</dd>

<dt id=""finishing""><dfn>finishing</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finishing_(textiles)"" title=""Finishing (textiles)"">Finishing</a> refers to any process performed on yarn or fabric after weaving to improve the look, performance, or ""hand"" (feel) of the finished textile.<sup about=""#mwt266"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPicken1957130_17-0"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{""group"":"""",""name"":""FOOTNOTEPicken1957130""},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPicken1957130-17""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPicken1957130-17"" id=""mwcw""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwdA""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwdQ"">[</span>17<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwdg"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""fishnet""><dfn>fishnet</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishnet_(material)"" title=""Fishnet (material)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Fishnet</a> is a material with an open, diamond shaped knit.</dd>

<dt id=""flannel""><dfn>flannel</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flannel"" title=""Flannel"">Flannel</a> is a cloth that is commonly used to make <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing"" title=""Clothing"">clothing</a> and bedsheets. It is usually made from either wool, wool and cotton, or wool and synthetic fabric.</dd>

<dt id=""flax""><dfn>flax</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flax"" title=""Flax"">Flax</a> fiber is soft, lustrous and flexible. It is stronger than cotton fiber but less elastic. The best grades are used for linen <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabrics"" title=""Fabrics"" class=""mw-redirect"">fabrics</a> such as damasks, lace and sheeting. Coarser grades are used for the manufacturing of twine and rope. </dd>

<dt id=""frieze""><dfn>frieze</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frieze_(textile)"" title=""Frieze (textile)"">Frieze</a> is a coarse woollen cloth with a nap on one side, that was raised by scrubbing it to raise curls of fiber (<a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language"" title=""French language"">French</a>: <i lang=""fr"">frisé</i><link rel=""mw:PageProp/Category"" href=""./Category:Articles_containing_French-language_text""/>). In the 19th century rough cheap frieze was made of wool mixed with shoddy (see Shoddy).</dd>

<dt id=""fulling""><dfn>fulling</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulling"" title=""Fulling"">Fulling</a> is a step in clothmaking which involves the cleansing of cloth (particularly wool) to get rid of oils, dirt, and other impurities.</dd>

<dt id=""fustian""><dfn>fustian</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fustian"" title=""Fustian"">Fustian</a> is a type of heavy twilled woven cotton fabrics, chiefly prepared for menswear. Usually dyed in a dark shade. Declined in popularity from 1813, being replaced by harder wearing and better quality wool cloths.</dd>

</dl>
