</section><section data-mw-section-id=""7"" id=""mweQ"" aria-labelledby=""G""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""G"">G</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7"" title=""Edit section: G""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt283"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mweg"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""gabardine""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Gabardine]] is a tough, tightly woven fabric often used to make [[suit (clothing)|suit]]s, [[overcoat]]s and [[trousers]]. The fiber used to make the fabric is traditionally worsted (a woolen yarn), but may also be cotton, synthetic or mixed. The fabric is smooth on one side and has a diagonally ribbed surface on the other.""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""gauge""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[Gauge (knitting)|gauge]] is a set number of rows per inch (in knitting) or the [[thread-count]] of a woven fabric that helps the knitter determine whether they have the right size [[knitting needles]] or a weaver if the cloth is tight enough.""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""ganté""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Ganté is a cloth made from cotton or [[tow (fibre)|tow]] warp and jute weft. It is largely used for bags for sugar and similar material, and has the appearance of a fine hessian cloth.&lt;ref>{{EB1911|inline=y |wstitle=Ganté |volume=11 |page=454}}&lt;/ref>""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""gauze""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Gauze]] is a very light, sheer, fine woven fabric.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Genova velvet""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A type of velvet where in Jacquard patterns are woven into the ground fabric and where the [[pile (fabric)|pile]] is made of a combination of cut and uncut (loop) pile. This fabric is also known as Venetian velvet, or more generally, as épinglé velvet. In the actual terminology of furnishing fabrics it is mostly named with its French name {{lang|fr|[[velours de Gênes]]}}.\nThis kind of fabric is made on a wire loom or [[épinglé loom]].""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""geotextile""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[geotextile]] is a synthetic permeable textile.""}},""i"":12}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""gingham""}},""i"":13}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Gingham]] is a fabric made from dyed cotton yarn.""}},""i"":14}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""glass fiber""}},""i"":15}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Fiberglass]] is material made from extremely fine fibers of [[glass]]. It is widely used in the manufacture of [[Thermal insulation|insulation]] and textiles.""}},""i"":16}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""gossamer""}},""i"":17}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A gossamer is a very light, sheer, [[gauze]]-like fabric, popular for [[white wedding]] dresses and decorations.&lt;ref>{{cite EB1911|wstitle=Gossamer |volume=12 |page=286 }}&lt;/ref>""}},""i"":18}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""grogram""}},""i"":19}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Grogram, also known as [[grosgrain]], is a coarse fabric of silk mixed with wool or with mohair and often stiffened with [[natural gum|gum]].""}},""i"":20}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":21}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt283"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt283"" id=""mwew"">

<dt id=""gabardine""><dfn>gabardine</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabardine"" title=""Gabardine"">Gabardine</a> is a tough, tightly woven fabric often used to make <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suit_(clothing)"" title=""Suit (clothing)"" class=""mw-redirect"">suits</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overcoat"" title=""Overcoat"">overcoats</a> and <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trousers"" title=""Trousers"">trousers</a>. The fiber used to make the fabric is traditionally worsted (a woolen yarn), but may also be cotton, synthetic or mixed. The fabric is smooth on one side and has a diagonally ribbed surface on the other.</dd>

<dt id=""gauge""><dfn>gauge</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauge_(knitting)"" title=""Gauge (knitting)"" class=""mw-redirect"">gauge</a> is a set number of rows per inch (in knitting) or the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thread-count"" title=""Thread-count"" class=""mw-redirect"">thread-count</a> of a woven fabric that helps the knitter determine whether they have the right size <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knitting_needles"" title=""Knitting needles"" class=""mw-redirect"">knitting needles</a> or a weaver if the cloth is tight enough.</dd>

<dt id=""ganté""><dfn>ganté</dfn></dt>
<dd>Ganté is a cloth made from cotton or <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tow_(fibre)"" title=""Tow (fibre)"">tow</a> warp and jute weft. It is largely used for bags for sugar and similar material, and has the appearance of a fine hessian cloth.<sup about=""#mwt293"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-18"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-18""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-18"" id=""mwfA""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwfQ""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwfg"">[</span>18<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwfw"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""gauze""><dfn>gauze</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauze"" title=""Gauze"">Gauze</a> is a very light, sheer, fine woven fabric.</dd>

<dt id=""genova_velvet""><dfn>Genova velvet</dfn></dt>
<dd>A type of velvet where in Jacquard patterns are woven into the ground fabric and where the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pile_(fabric)"" title=""Pile (fabric)"" class=""mw-redirect"">pile</a> is made of a combination of cut and uncut (loop) pile. This fabric is also known as Venetian velvet, or more generally, as épinglé velvet. In the actual terminology of furnishing fabrics it is mostly named with its French name <span title=""French-language text""><i lang=""fr""><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velours_de_Gênes?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Velours de Gênes (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Velours de Gênes""]}}'>velours de Gênes</a></i></span><link rel=""mw:PageProp/Category"" href=""./Category:Articles_containing_French-language_text""/>.
This kind of fabric is made on a wire loom or <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Épinglé_loom?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Épinglé loom (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Épinglé loom""]}}'>épinglé loom</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""geotextile""><dfn>geotextile</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geotextile"" title=""Geotextile"">geotextile</a> is a synthetic permeable textile.</dd>

<dt id=""gingham""><dfn>gingham</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gingham"" title=""Gingham"">Gingham</a> is a fabric made from dyed cotton yarn.</dd>

<dt id=""glass_fiber""><dfn>glass fiber</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiberglass"" title=""Fiberglass"">Fiberglass</a> is material made from extremely fine fibers of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glass"" title=""Glass"">glass</a>. It is widely used in the manufacture of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_insulation"" title=""Thermal insulation"">insulation</a> and textiles.</dd>

<dt id=""gossamer""><dfn>gossamer</dfn></dt>
<dd>A gossamer is a very light, sheer, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauze"" title=""Gauze"">gauze</a>-like fabric, popular for <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_wedding"" title=""White wedding"">white wedding</a> dresses and decorations.<sup about=""#mwt310"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-19"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-19""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-19"" id=""mwgA""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwgQ""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwgg"">[</span>19<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwgw"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""grogram""><dfn>grogram</dfn></dt>
<dd>Grogram, also known as <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grosgrain"" title=""Grosgrain"">grosgrain</a>, is a coarse fabric of silk mixed with wool or with mohair and often stiffened with <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gum"" title=""Natural gum"">gum</a>.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt316"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwhA"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt316"" id=""mwhQ""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt317"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""8"" id=""mwhg"" aria-labelledby=""H""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""H"">H</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8"" title=""Edit section: H""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt318"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwhw"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""hand""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The hand of a fabric is how it feels to the touch.&lt;ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.nytimes.com/1991/09/15/magazine/next-weave.html |title=Next Weave |last=La Ferla |first=Ruth |work=New York Times |date=15 September 1991 |access-date=20 August 2018}}&lt;/ref>""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""heald""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[heald]] is a loom component also called heddle or harness, used to separate warp yarns for passage of the weft. Commonly made of cord or wire. Minimum two healds are required to weave a fabric with warp and weft in a loom.""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""heddle""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[heddle]] is a common loom component, used to separate warp yarns for passage of the weft. Commonly made of cord or wire.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""hem""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""no"":{""wt"":""1""},""1"":{""wt"":""To [[hem]] a piece of cloth (in sewing), a garment worker folds up a cut edge, folds it up again, and then sews it down. The process of hemming thus completely encloses the cut edge in cloth, so that it cannot ravel.""}},""i"":8}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""no"":{""wt"":""2""},""1"":{""wt"":""The edge of cloth hemmed in this manner.""}},""i"":9}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""hemp""}},""i"":10}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The main uses of [[hemp]] fiber are rope, sacking, carpet, nets and webbing. Hemp is also being used in increasing quantities in paper manufacturing. The cellulose content is about 70%.""}},""i"":11}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Holland cloth""}},""i"":12}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Holland cloth]] (or Holland) {{en dash}} one of different types of plain linen or cotton fabric, sometimes glazed.""}},""i"":13}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""huckaback""}},""i"":14}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Huckaback]] is a type of coarse absorbent cotton or linen fabric used for making [[towel]]s.&lt;ref>{{Cite EB1911|wstitle=Huckaback}}&lt;/ref>""}},""i"":15}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":16}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt318"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt318"" id=""mwiA"">

<dt id=""hand""><dfn>hand</dfn></dt>
<dd>The hand of a fabric is how it feels to the touch.<sup about=""#mwt324"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-20"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-20""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-20"" id=""mwiQ""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwig""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwiw"">[</span>20<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwjA"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""heald""><dfn>heald</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heald"" title=""Heald"">heald</a> is a loom component also called heddle or harness, used to separate warp yarns for passage of the weft. Commonly made of cord or wire. Minimum two healds are required to weave a fabric with warp and weft in a loom.</dd>


<dt id=""heddle""><dfn>heddle</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heddle"" title=""Heddle"">heddle</a> is a common loom component, used to separate warp yarns for passage of the weft. Commonly made of cord or wire.</dd>

<dt id=""hem""><dfn>hem</dfn></dt>
<dd>1.<span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span>To <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hem"" title=""Hem"">hem</a> a piece of cloth (in sewing), a garment worker folds up a cut edge, folds it up again, and then sews it down. The process of hemming thus completely encloses the cut edge in cloth, so that it cannot ravel.</dd>
<dd>2.<span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span>The edge of cloth hemmed in this manner.</dd>

<dt id=""hemp""><dfn>hemp</dfn></dt>
<dd>The main uses of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemp"" title=""Hemp"">hemp</a> fiber are rope, sacking, carpet, nets and webbing. Hemp is also being used in increasing quantities in paper manufacturing. The cellulose content is about 70%.</dd>

<dt id=""holland_cloth""><dfn>Holland cloth</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holland_cloth"" title=""Holland cloth"">Holland cloth</a> (or Holland) – one of different types of plain linen or cotton fabric, sometimes glazed.</dd>

<dt id=""huckaback""><dfn>huckaback</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huckaback"" title=""Huckaback"">Huckaback</a> is a type of coarse absorbent cotton or linen fabric used for making <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Towel"" title=""Towel"">towels</a>.<sup about=""#mwt342"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-21"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-21""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-21"" id=""mwjQ""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwjg""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwjw"">[</span>21<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwkA"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt346"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwkQ"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt346"" id=""mwkg""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt347"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#A"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">A</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#B"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">B</a></li>

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""9"" id=""mwkw"" aria-labelledby=""I""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""I"">I</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9"" title=""Edit section: I""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt348"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwlA"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""ikat""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Ikat]] is a style of weaving that uses a [[tie-dye]] process on either the warp or weft before the threads are woven to create a pattern or design.  A &apos;&apos;&apos;double ikat&apos;&apos;&apos; is when both the warp &apos;&apos;and&apos;&apos; the weft are tie-dyed before weaving.""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""intarsia""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":"" [[Intarsia (knitting)|Intarsia]] is a knitting technique used to create patterns with multiple colours.""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""interfacing""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A type of material used on the unseen or \""wrong\"" side of fabrics in sewing.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":7}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt348"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt348"" id=""mwlQ"">

<dt id=""ikat""><dfn>ikat</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikat"" title=""Ikat"">Ikat</a> is a style of weaving that uses a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tie-dye"" title=""Tie-dye"">tie-dye</a> process on either the warp or weft before the threads are woven to create a pattern or design.  A <b>double ikat</b> is when both the warp <i>and</i> the weft are tie-dyed before weaving.</dd>

<dt id=""intarsia""><dfn>intarsia</dfn></dt>
<dd> <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intarsia_(knitting)"" title=""Intarsia (knitting)"">Intarsia</a> is a knitting technique used to create patterns with multiple colours.</dd>

<dt id=""interfacing""><dfn>interfacing</dfn></dt>
<dd>A type of material used on the unseen or ""wrong"" side of fabrics in sewing.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt357"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwlg"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt357"" id=""mwlw""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt358"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

<div style=""margin:auto; "">

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""10"" id=""mwmA"" aria-labelledby=""J""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""J"">J</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10"" title=""Edit section: J""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt359"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwmQ"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Jacquard""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""no"":{""wt"":""1""},""1"":{""wt"":""The fabric made on a [[Jacquard loom]].""}},""i"":2}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""no"":{""wt"":""2""},""1"":{""wt"":""The attachment for a hand loom or power loom that allows warp threads to be individually controlled. This enables the production of Jacquard fabric.""}},""i"":3}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Jacquard loom""}},""i"":4}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The [[Jacquard loom]] was the first machine to use [[punched card]]s. It uses punched cards to control the pattern being woven. It is a form of dobby loom, where individual harnesses can be raised and lowered independently.""}},""i"":5}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""jamdani""}},""i"":6}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Jamdani]] is a kind of fine cloth made in [[Bangladesh]].""}},""i"":7}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""jute""}},""i"":8}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Jute]] is a long, soft, shiny plant fiber that can be spun into coarse, strong threads. Jute is one of the cheapest natural fibers, and is second only to cotton in amount produced and variety of uses. Jute fibers are composed primarily of the plant materials cellulose and [[lignin]].""}},""i"":9}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":10}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt359"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt359"" id=""mwmg"">

<dt id=""jacquard""><dfn>Jacquard</dfn></dt>
<dd>1.<span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span>The fabric made on a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacquard_loom"" title=""Jacquard loom"" class=""mw-redirect"">Jacquard loom</a>.</dd>
<dd>2.<span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span>The attachment for a hand loom or power loom that allows warp threads to be individually controlled. This enables the production of Jacquard fabric.</dd>

<dt id=""jacquard_loom""><dfn>Jacquard loom</dfn></dt>
<dd>The <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacquard_loom"" title=""Jacquard loom"" class=""mw-redirect"">Jacquard loom</a> was the first machine to use <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punched_card"" title=""Punched card"">punched cards</a>. It uses punched cards to control the pattern being woven. It is a form of dobby loom, where individual harnesses can be raised and lowered independently.</dd>

<dt id=""jamdani""><dfn>jamdani</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamdani"" title=""Jamdani"">Jamdani</a> is a kind of fine cloth made in <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh"" title=""Bangladesh"">Bangladesh</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""jute""><dfn>jute</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jute"" title=""Jute"">Jute</a> is a long, soft, shiny plant fiber that can be spun into coarse, strong threads. Jute is one of the cheapest natural fibers, and is second only to cotton in amount produced and variety of uses. Jute fibers are composed primarily of the plant materials cellulose and <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lignin"" title=""Lignin"">lignin</a>.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt371"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwmw"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt371"" id=""mwnA""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt372"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""11"" id=""mwnQ"" aria-labelledby=""K""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""K"">K</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11"" title=""Edit section: K""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt373"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwng"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""knitting""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Knitting]] is the process of inter-looping of [[yarn]]s or inter-meshing of loops.""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""knit fabrics""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Knits|Knit]] fabrics are fabrics that were produced through the process of [[knitting]].""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""knitting needle gauge""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[knitting needle]] gauge is used to determine the size of a knitting needle. Some also double for crochet hooks. Most needles come with the size written on the needle, but many needles (like double-pointed needles) tend to not be labeled. Also, with wear and time the label often wears off.\n\nNeedle gauges can be made of any material, and are often made for metal and plastic. They tend to be about 3 by 5 inches. They contain holes of various sizes, and often have a ruler along the edge for determining the gauge of a sample.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":7}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt373"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt373"" id=""mwnw"">

<dt id=""knitting""><dfn>knitting</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knitting"" title=""Knitting"">Knitting</a> is the process of inter-looping of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarn"" title=""Yarn"">yarns</a> or inter-meshing of loops.</dd>

<dt id=""knit_fabrics""><dfn>knit fabrics</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knits"" title=""Knits"" class=""mw-redirect"">Knit</a> fabrics are fabrics that were produced through the process of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knitting"" title=""Knitting"">knitting</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""knitting_needle_gauge""><dfn>knitting needle gauge</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knitting_needle"" title=""Knitting needle"">knitting needle</a> gauge is used to determine the size of a knitting needle. Some also double for crochet hooks. Most needles come with the size written on the needle, but many needles (like double-pointed needles) tend to not be labeled. Also, with wear and time the label often wears off.

Needle gauges can be made of any material, and are often made for metal and plastic. They tend to be about 3 by 5 inches. They contain holes of various sizes, and often have a ruler along the edge for determining the gauge of a sample.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt382"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwoA"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt382"" id=""mwoQ""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt383"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""12"" id=""mwog"" aria-labelledby=""L""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""L"">L</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12"" title=""Edit section: L""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt384"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwow"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""lace""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The creation of [[lace]] is an ancient craft. [[Linens]] are fabric household goods, such as pillowcases and towels, many of which were historically made from linen cloth.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""lining""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Lining (sewing)|Lining]]""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""loden""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Loden (material)|Loden]] is water-resistant material for clothing made from sheep wool.""}},""i"":12}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""loft""}},""i"":13}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""An indication of thickness of a textile when not under compression. High loft implies that the fabric contains more air than fibres, has a low overall density and is likely to be a good insulator due to a large volume of constrained air. ""}},""i"":14}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""loom""}},""i"":15}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[loom]] is a machine used for weaving fabric.""}},""i"":16}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""lucet""}},""i"":17}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Lucet]] is a method of cordmaking or braiding which is believed to date back to the [[Viking Age]].  Lucet cord is square, strong, and slightly springy.  It closely resembles knitted I-cord or the cord produced on a knitting spool.  Lucet cord is formed by a series of loops, and will therefore unravel if cut.""}},""i"":18}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":19}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt384"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt384"" id=""mwpA"">

<dt id=""lace""><dfn>lace</dfn></dt>
<dd>The creation of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lace"" title=""Lace"">lace</a> is an ancient craft. A lace fabric is lightweight openwork fabric, patterned, either by machine or by hand, with open holes in the work. The holes can be formed via removal of threads or cloth from a previously woven fabric, but more often lace is built up from a single thread and the open spaces are created as part of the lace fabric.</dd>

<dt id=""lamé""><dfn>lamé</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamé_(fabric)"" title=""Lamé (fabric)"">Lamé</a> is a type of brocaded clothing fabric with inwoven metal threads, typically of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold"" title=""Gold"">gold</a> or <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver"" title=""Silver"">silver</a>, giving it a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_color"" title=""Metallic color"">metallic</a> sheen.</dd>

<dt id=""lawn""><dfn>lawn</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawn_(fabric)"" title=""Lawn (fabric)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Lawn</a> is a fine linen or cotton cloth.</dd>

<dt id=""linen""><dfn>linen</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linen"" title=""Linen"">Linen</a> is a material made from the fibers of the flax plant. <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linens"" title=""Linens"">Linens</a> are fabric household goods, such as pillowcases and towels, many of which were historically made from linen cloth.</dd>

<dt id=""lining""><dfn>lining</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lining_(sewing)"" title=""Lining (sewing)"">Lining</a></dd>

<dt id=""loden""><dfn>loden</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loden_(material)"" title=""Loden (material)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Loden</a> is water-resistant material for clothing made from sheep wool.</dd>

<dt id=""loft""><dfn>loft</dfn></dt>
<dd>An indication of thickness of a textile when not under compression. High loft implies that the fabric contains more air than fibres, has a low overall density and is likely to be a good insulator due to a large volume of constrained air. </dd>

<dt id=""loom""><dfn>loom</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loom"" title=""Loom"">loom</a> is a machine used for weaving fabric.</dd>

<dt id=""lucet""><dfn>lucet</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucet"" title=""Lucet"">Lucet</a> is a method of cordmaking or braiding which is believed to date back to the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking_Age"" title=""Viking Age"">Viking Age</a>.  Lucet cord is square, strong, and slightly springy.  It closely resembles knitted I-cord or the cord produced on a knitting spool.  Lucet cord is formed by a series of loops, and will therefore unravel if cut.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt405"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwpQ"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt405"" id=""mwpg""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt406"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""13"" id=""mwpw"" aria-labelledby=""M""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""M"">M</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13"" title=""Edit section: M""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt407"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwqA"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""macramé""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Macramé]] (also &apos;&apos;macrame&apos;&apos;) is a form of textile-making using [[knot]]ting rather than weaving or knitting. Its primary knots are the [[Reef knot|square knot]] and forms of hitching ([[full hitch]] and double [[half hitch]]es).""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""mercerized cotton""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Mercerized cotton|Mercerization]] is a treatment for cotton fabric and thread mostly employed to give cotton a lustrous appearance.""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""merino""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Merino]] is the [[Spanish language|Spanish]] name for a breed of sheep, and hence applied to a woolen fabric.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""mesh""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[mesh]] is similar to fabric or a [[spider web|web]] in that it has many connected or weaved pieces.  In clothing, a mesh is often defined as fabric that has a large number of closely spaced holes, such as is common practice for modern sports [[jersey (clothing)|jersey]]s.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""metallic fiber""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Metallic fiber]]s are fibers used in textiles which are either composed of metal, or fibers of other materials with a metal coating.\nTheir uses include decoration and the reduction of [[static electricity]].""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Microfiber""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Fibers with strands thinner than one [[denier (measure)|denier]]. Fabrics made with microfibers are exceptionally soft and hold their shape well.""}},""i"":12}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""millinery""}},""i"":13}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""no"":{""wt"":""1""},""1"":{""wt"":""[[Hatmaking|Millinery]] the profession or business of designing, making, or selling hats for women.""}},""i"":14}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""no"":{""wt"":""2""},""1"":{""wt"":""Women&apos;s [[hat]]s and other articles sold by a milliner.""}},""i"":15}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""mockado""}},""i"":16}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Mockado]] is a woollen [[pile (textile)|pile]] fabric made in imitation of silk velvet.{{sfn|Jenkins|2003|p=434}}{{sfn|Cox|Dannehl|2007|loc=\""Mockado\""}}{{sfn|Montgomery|2007|p=295}}""}},""i"":17}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""modal""}},""i"":18}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Modal (textile)|Modal]] is a [[cellulose fiber]] made by spinning reconstituted cellulose from [[beech]] trees.""}},""i"":19}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""mohair""}},""i"":20}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Mohair]] is a silk-like fabric made from the [[hair]] of the Angora goat. It is durable, light and warm, although some people find it uncomfortably itchy.""}},""i"":21}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""mungo""}},""i"":22}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Fibrous woollen material generated from waste fabric, particularly tightly woven cloths and rags. See also {{gli|shoddy}}.""}},""i"":23}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""muslin""}},""i"":24}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Muslin]] is a type of finely woven cotton fabric, introduced to [[Europe]] from the [[Middle East]] in the 17th century.  It was named for the city where it was first made, [[Mosul]] in what is now [[Iraq]].""}},""i"":25}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":26}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt407"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt407"" id=""mwqQ"">

<dt id=""macramé""><dfn>macramé</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macramé"" title=""Macramé"">Macramé</a> (also <i>macrame</i>) is a form of textile-making using <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knot"" title=""Knot"">knotting</a> rather than weaving or knitting. Its primary knots are the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reef_knot"" title=""Reef knot"">square knot</a> and forms of hitching (<a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_hitch?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Full hitch (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Full hitch""]}}'>full hitch</a> and double <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half_hitch"" title=""Half hitch"">half hitches</a>).</dd>

<dt id=""mercerized_cotton""><dfn>mercerized cotton</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercerized_cotton"" title=""Mercerized cotton"" class=""mw-redirect"">Mercerization</a> is a treatment for cotton fabric and thread mostly employed to give cotton a lustrous appearance.</dd>

<dt id=""merino""><dfn>merino</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merino"" title=""Merino"">Merino</a> is the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language"" title=""Spanish language"">Spanish</a> name for a breed of sheep, and hence applied to a woolen fabric.</dd>

<dt id=""mesh""><dfn>mesh</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesh"" title=""Mesh"">mesh</a> is similar to fabric or a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spider_web"" title=""Spider web"">web</a> in that it has many connected or weaved pieces.  In clothing, a mesh is often defined as fabric that has a large number of closely spaced holes, such as is common practice for modern sports <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_(clothing)"" title=""Jersey (clothing)"" class=""mw-redirect"">jerseys</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""metallic_fiber""><dfn>metallic fiber</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallic_fiber"" title=""Metallic fiber"">Metallic fibers</a> are fibers used in textiles which are either composed of metal, or fibers of other materials with a metal coating.
Their uses include decoration and the reduction of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Static_electricity"" title=""Static electricity"">static electricity</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""microfiber""><dfn>Microfiber</dfn></dt>
<dd>Fibers with strands thinner than one <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denier_(measure)"" title=""Denier (measure)"" class=""mw-redirect"">denier</a>. Fabrics made with microfibers are exceptionally soft and hold their shape well.</dd>

<dt id=""millinery""><dfn>millinery</dfn></dt>
<dd>1.<span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hatmaking"" title=""Hatmaking"">Millinery</a> the profession or business of designing, making, or selling hats for women.</dd>
<dd>2.<span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span>Women's <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hat"" title=""Hat"">hats</a> and other articles sold by a milliner.</dd>

<dt id=""mockado""><dfn>mockado</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mockado"" title=""Mockado"">Mockado</a> is a woollen <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pile_(textile)"" title=""Pile (textile)"">pile</a> fabric made in imitation of silk velvet.<sup about=""#mwt426"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJenkins2003434_22-0"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{""group"":"""",""name"":""FOOTNOTEJenkins2003434""},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEJenkins2003434-22""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJenkins2003434-22"" id=""mwqg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwqw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwrA"">[</span>22<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwrQ"">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about=""#mwt428"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id='cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoxDannehl2007""Mockado""_23-0' rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{""group"":"""",""name"":""FOOTNOTECoxDannehl2007\""Mockado\""""},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxDannehl2007\""Mockado\""-23""}}'><a href='#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxDannehl2007""Mockado""-23' id=""mwrg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwrw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwsA"">[</span>23<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwsQ"">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about=""#mwt430"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontgomery2007295_24-0"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{""group"":"""",""name"":""FOOTNOTEMontgomery2007295""},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontgomery2007295-24""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontgomery2007295-24"" id=""mwsg""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwsw""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwtA"">[</span>24<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwtQ"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""modal""><dfn>modal</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modal_(textile)"" title=""Modal (textile)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Modal</a> is a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellulose_fiber"" title=""Cellulose fiber"">cellulose fiber</a> made by spinning reconstituted cellulose from <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beech"" title=""Beech"">beech</a> trees.</dd>

<dt id=""mohair""><dfn>mohair</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohair"" title=""Mohair"">Mohair</a> is a silk-like fabric made from the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hair"" title=""Hair"">hair</a> of the Angora goat. It is durable, light and warm, although some people find it uncomfortably itchy.</dd>

<dt id=""mungo""><dfn>mungo</dfn></dt>
<dd>Fibrous woollen material generated from waste fabric, particularly tightly woven cloths and rags. See also <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#shoddy"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment""><span title=""See entry on this page at § shoddy"" class=""glossary-link-internal "" style=""border-bottom:1px dashed #86a1ff;color:var(--color-base,#202122);"">shoddy</span></a>.</dd>

<dt id=""muslin""><dfn>muslin</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslin"" title=""Muslin"">Muslin</a> is a type of finely woven cotton fabric, introduced to <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe"" title=""Europe"">Europe</a> from the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East"" title=""Middle East"">Middle East</a> in the 17th century.  It was named for the city where it was first made, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosul"" title=""Mosul"">Mosul</a> in what is now <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq"" title=""Iraq"">Iraq</a>.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt441"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwtg"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt441"" id=""mwtw""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt442"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""14"" id=""mwuA"" aria-labelledby=""N""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""N"">N</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14"" title=""Edit section: N""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt443"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwuQ"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""nainsook""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Nainsook]] is a fine, soft muslin fabric, often used to make [[Infant clothing|babies clothing]].""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""nap""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[nap (textile)|Nap]] is the raised surface of certain cloth, such as flannel.""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""needlepoint""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Needlepoint]] is a form of canvas work created on a mesh canvas.  The stitching threads used may be wool, silk, or rarely cotton.  Stitches may be plain, covering just one mesh intersection with a single orientation, or fancy, such as [[Bargello (needlework)|Bargello]].  Plain stitches, known as [[Tent stitch]]es, may be worked as [[basketweave]] or half cross.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""needlework""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Needlework]] is another term for the [[handicraft]] of decorative sewing and textile arts.  Anything that uses a [[Sewing needle|needle]] for construction can be called needlework.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""net""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Net (device)|Net]] is a device made by fibers woven in a grid-like structure, as in [[fishing net]], a [[soccer]] goal, a [[butterfly net]], or the court divider in [[tennis]].""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""non-woven fabric""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Non-woven textiles]] are those which are neither woven nor knit, for example felt.  Non-wovens are typically not strong (unless reinforced by a backing), and do not stretch.  They are cheap to manufacture.""}},""i"":12}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""novelty yarn""}},""i"":13}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Novelty yarn]].""}},""i"":14}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""nylon""}},""i"":15}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Nylon]] is a [[synthetic polymer]], a [[Plastic#Nylon|plastic]]. Nylon fibers are used to make many synthetic fabrics and women&apos;s stockings.""}},""i"":16}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":17}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt443"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt443"" id=""mwug"">

<dt id=""nainsook""><dfn>nainsook</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nainsook"" title=""Nainsook"">Nainsook</a> is a fine, soft muslin fabric, often used to make <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_clothing"" title=""Infant clothing"">babies clothing</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""nap""><dfn>nap</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nap_(textile)"" title=""Nap (textile)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Nap</a> is the raised surface of certain cloth, such as flannel.</dd>

<dt id=""needlepoint""><dfn>needlepoint</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Needlepoint"" title=""Needlepoint"">Needlepoint</a> is a form of canvas work created on a mesh canvas.  The stitching threads used may be wool, silk, or rarely cotton.  Stitches may be plain, covering just one mesh intersection with a single orientation, or fancy, such as <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bargello_(needlework)"" title=""Bargello (needlework)"">Bargello</a>.  Plain stitches, known as <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tent_stitch"" title=""Tent stitch"">Tent stitches</a>, may be worked as <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketweave"" title=""Basketweave"">basketweave</a> or half cross.</dd>

<dt id=""needlework""><dfn>needlework</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Needlework"" title=""Needlework"">Needlework</a> is another term for the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handicraft"" title=""Handicraft"">handicraft</a> of decorative sewing and textile arts.  Anything that uses a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sewing_needle"" title=""Sewing needle"">needle</a> for construction can be called needlework.</dd>

<dt id=""net""><dfn>net</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_(device)"" title=""Net (device)"">Net</a> is a device made by fibers woven in a grid-like structure, as in <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing_net"" title=""Fishing net"">fishing net</a>, a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soccer"" title=""Soccer"" class=""mw-redirect"">soccer</a> goal, a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butterfly_net"" title=""Butterfly net"">butterfly net</a>, or the court divider in <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennis"" title=""Tennis"">tennis</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""non-woven_fabric""><dfn>non-woven fabric</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-woven_textiles"" title=""Non-woven textiles"" class=""mw-redirect"">Non-woven textiles</a> are those which are neither woven nor knit, for example felt.  Non-wovens are typically not strong (unless reinforced by a backing), and do not stretch.  They are cheap to manufacture.</dd>

<dt id=""novelty_yarn""><dfn>novelty yarn</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novelty_yarn"" title=""Novelty yarn"" class=""mw-redirect"">Novelty yarn</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""nylon""><dfn>nylon</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nylon"" title=""Nylon"">Nylon</a> is a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic_polymer"" title=""Synthetic polymer"" class=""mw-redirect"">synthetic polymer</a>, a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plastic#Nylon"" title=""Plastic"">plastic</a>. Nylon fibers are used to make many synthetic fabrics and women's stockings.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt462"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwuw"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt462"" id=""mwvA""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt463"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

<div style=""margin:auto; "">

<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#T"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">T</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#U"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">U</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#V"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">V</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#W"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">W</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#X"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">X</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#Y"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">Y</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#Z"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">Z</a></li>
<li><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#External_links"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">External links</a></li></ul>
</div></div></div></div>

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""15"" id=""mwvQ"" aria-labelledby=""O""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""O"">O</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15"" title=""Edit section: O""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt464"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwvg"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""oil cloth""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Oil cloth]] was, traditionally, heavy cotton or linen cloth with a [[linseed oil]] coating: it was semi-waterproof. The most familiar use was for brightly printed kitchen tablecloths.&lt;ref>{{cite web|title=Printed Tablecloths|url=http://www.textiletown.co.uk/buy/Printed-Tablecloths.html|website=www.textiletown.co.uk|access-date=2 October 2016}}&lt;/ref> Dull-colored oilcloth was used for bedrolls, [[sou&apos;wester]]s, and tents. By the late 1950s, oilcloth became a synonym for [[Polyvinyl chloride|vinyl]] (polyvinyl chloride) bonded to either a flanneled cloth or a printed vinyl with a synthetic non-woven backing.""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""organdy""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Organdy]] or organdie is the sheerest cotton cloth made. Combed yarns contribute to its appearance. Its sheerness and crispness are the result of an acid finish on greige (unbleached) lawn goods. Because of its stiffness and fiber content, it is very prone to wrinkling.""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""organza""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Organza]] is a thin, plain weave, sheer fabric traditionally made from silk, the continuous filament of silkworms. Nowadays, though many organzas are woven with synthetic filament fibers such as polyester or nylon, the most luxurious organzas are still woven in silk.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":7}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt464"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt464"" id=""mwvw"">

<dt id=""oil_cloth""><dfn>oil cloth</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_cloth"" title=""Oil cloth"" class=""mw-redirect"">Oil cloth</a> was, traditionally, heavy cotton or linen cloth with a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linseed_oil"" title=""Linseed oil"">linseed oil</a> coating: it was semi-waterproof. The most familiar use was for brightly printed kitchen tablecloths.<sup about=""#mwt470"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-25"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-25""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-25"" id=""mwwA""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mwwQ""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwwg"">[</span>25<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mwww"">]</span></span></a></sup> Dull-colored oilcloth was used for bedrolls, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sou'wester"" title=""Sou'wester"">sou'westers</a>, and tents. By the late 1950s, oilcloth became a synonym for <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyvinyl_chloride"" title=""Polyvinyl chloride"">vinyl</a> (polyvinyl chloride) bonded to either a flanneled cloth or a printed vinyl with a synthetic non-woven backing.</dd>

<dt id=""organdy""><dfn>organdy</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organdy"" title=""Organdy"">Organdy</a> or organdie is the sheerest cotton cloth made. Combed yarns contribute to its appearance. Its sheerness and crispness are the result of an acid finish on greige (unbleached) lawn goods. Because of its stiffness and fiber content, it is very prone to wrinkling.</dd>

<dt id=""organza""><dfn>organza</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organza"" title=""Organza"">Organza</a> is a thin, plain weave, sheer fabric traditionally made from silk, the continuous filament of silkworms. Nowadays, though many organzas are woven with synthetic filament fibers such as polyester or nylon, the most luxurious organzas are still woven in silk.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt478"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwxA"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt478"" id=""mwxQ""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt479"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

weave""}},""i"":21}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Plain weave]]""}},""i"":22}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""plied yarn""}},""i"":23}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[plying|Plied]] yarn is yarn that has been plied, with the process fiber.""}},""i"":28}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""poplin""}},""i"":29}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Poplin]] </span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt480"" id=""mwyA"">

<dt id=""paisley""><dfn>paisley</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paisley_(design)"" title=""Paisley (design)"">Paisley</a> is a droplet-shaped vegetal motif, similar to half of the <i><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taijitu"" title=""Taijitu"">taijitu</a></i> symbol, the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India"" title=""India"">Indian</a> <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhi_tree"" title=""Bodhi tree"">bodhi tree leaf</a>, or the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mango"" title=""Mango"">mango tree</a>. The design originated in India and spread to <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scotland"" title=""Scotland"">Scotland</a> when British soldiers brought home cashmere shawls.</dd>

<dt id=""pashmina""><dfn>pashmina</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashmina"" title=""Pashmina"" class=""mw-redirect"">Pashmina</a> is the fiber obtained from pasmina goats in kashmir region, used for winter clothes and shawls.</dd>

<dt id=""patchwork""><dfn>patchwork</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patchwork"" title=""Patchwork"">Patchwork</a> is a form of needlework or craft that involves sewing together small pieces of fabric and stitching them together into a larger design, which is then usually quilted, or else tied together with pieces of yarn at regular intervals, a practice known as tying. Patchwork is traditionally 'pieced' by hand, but modern quiltmakers often use a sewing machine instead.</dd>

<dt id=""percale""><dfn>percale</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Percale"" title=""Percale"">Percale</a> refers to a closely woven, high thread count, cotton fabric often used for sheets and clothing.</dd>

<dt id=""persian_weave""><dfn>Persian weave</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_weave"" title=""Persian weave"">Persian weave</a> is a method of weave used in <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewelry"" title=""Jewelry"" class=""mw-redirect"">jewelry</a> and other art forms.</dd>

<dt id=""pile_knit""><dfn>pile knit</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pile_knit?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Pile knit (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Pile knit""]}}'>Pile knit</a></dd>

<dt id=""pile_weave""><dfn>pile weave</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pile_weave"" title=""Pile weave"">Pile weave</a></dd>

<dt id=""pile_wire""><dfn>pile wire</dfn></dt>
<dd>A steel rod which is inserted in between the base fabric and the pile ends in a pile fabric woven on a wire loom or épinglé loom.  The height and thickness of the rod determine the size of the loop. A pile wire can be a simple rod<span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span><span typeof=""mw:Entity"">–</span><span typeof=""mw:Entity""> </span>in which case the pile yarns will form a 'loop' pile. If the pile wire is equipped with a blade holder and cutting blade at the tip it will cut the pile loops during extraction thus producing cut pile.</dd>

<dt id=""pill""><dfn>pill</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pill_(textile)"" title=""Pill (textile)"">Pill</a></dd>

<dt id=""plaid""><dfn>plaid</dfn></dt>
<dd>From a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scots_language"" title=""Scots language"">Scots language</a> word meaning <i>blanket</i>, plaid usually referring to patterned woollen cloth otherwise known as <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tartan"" title=""Tartan"">tartan</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""plain_weave""><dfn>plain weave</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plain_weave"" title=""Plain weave"">Plain weave</a></dd>

<dt id=""plied_yarn""><dfn>plied yarn</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plying"" title=""Plying"">Plied</a> yarn is yarn that has been plied, with the process called plying.</dd>

<dt id=""plush""><dfn>plush</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plush"" title=""Plush"" class=""mw-redirect"">Plush</a> is a fabric having a cut nap or pile the same as fustian or velvet.</dd>

<dt id=""polyester""><dfn>polyester</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyester"" title=""Polyester"">Polyester</a> is a synthetic fiber.</dd>

<dt id=""poplin""><dfn>poplin</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poplin"" title=""Poplin"">Poplin</a>  is a heavy, durable fabric that has a ribbed appearance. It is made with wool, cotton, silk, rayon, or any mixture of these. The ribs run across the fabric from selvage to selvage.  They are formed by using coarse filling yarns in a plain weave.</dd>

<dt id=""punched""><dfn>punched</dfn></dt>
<dd>A type of fabric structure that gives different holes or figured textures.</dd>

<dt id=""purl_stitch""><dfn>purl stitch</dfn></dt>
<dd>A commonly used stitch in knitting.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt517"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwyQ"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt517"" id=""mwyg""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt518"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">
