</section><section data-mw-section-id=""17"" id=""mwyw"" aria-labelledby=""Q""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""Q"">Q</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17"" title=""Edit section: Q""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt519"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwzA"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""qalamkari""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Qalamkari]] (also &apos;&apos;kalamkari&apos;&apos;) is a type of hand-painted or block-printed textile, produced in various places in India.""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""qiviut""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Qiviut]] is the wool of the [[musk ox]].""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""quilt""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Quilting]] is a method of sewing or tying two layers of cloth with a layer of insulating batting in between. A bed covering or similar large rectangular piece of quilting work is called a [[quilt]].""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":7}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt519"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt519"" id=""mwzQ"">

<dt id=""qalamkari""><dfn>qalamkari</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qalamkari"" title=""Qalamkari"" class=""mw-redirect"">Qalamkari</a> (also <i>kalamkari</i>) is a type of hand-painted or block-printed textile, produced in various places in India.</dd>

<dt id=""qiviut""><dfn>qiviut</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiviut"" title=""Qiviut"">Qiviut</a> is the wool of the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musk_ox"" title=""Musk ox"" class=""mw-redirect"">musk ox</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""quilt""><dfn>quilt</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quilting"" title=""Quilting"">Quilting</a> is a method of sewing or tying two layers of cloth with a layer of insulating batting in between. A bed covering or similar large rectangular piece of quilting work is called a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quilt"" title=""Quilt"">quilt</a>.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt528"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mwzg"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt528"" id=""mwzw""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt529"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""18"" id=""mw0A"" aria-labelledby=""R""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""R"">R</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18"" title=""Edit section: R""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt530"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mw0Q"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""rayon""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Rayon]] is a fiber made of processed [[cellulose]].  Cellulose fibers from [[wood]] or cotton are dissolved in [[alkali]] to make a solution called viscose, which is then extruded through a nozzle, or spinneret, into an [[acid]] bath to reconvert the viscose into cellulose.  A similar process, using a slit instead of a hole, is used to make [[cellophane]].""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""rib knit""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Rib knit]]""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""rolag""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[rolag]] is a loose woolen roll of fibers that results from using handcards.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""roving""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[roving]] is a long rope of fibers where all of the fibers have been [[Carding|carded]] to lie parallel to each another.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""rug""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[Carpet|rug]] is a form of carpet. It is usually smaller than a carpet. See also: [[rug making]]""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":11}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt530"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt530"" id=""mw0g"">

<dt id=""rayon""><dfn>rayon</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rayon"" title=""Rayon"">Rayon</a> is a fiber made of processed <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellulose"" title=""Cellulose"">cellulose</a>.  Cellulose fibers from <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood"" title=""Wood"">wood</a> or cotton are dissolved in <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali"" title=""Alkali"">alkali</a> to make a solution called viscose, which is then extruded through a nozzle, or spinneret, into an <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid"" title=""Acid"">acid</a> bath to reconvert the viscose into cellulose.  A similar process, using a slit instead of a hole, is used to make <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellophane"" title=""Cellophane"">cellophane</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""rib_knit""><dfn>rib knit</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rib_knit"" title=""Rib knit"" class=""mw-redirect"">Rib knit</a></dd>

<dt id=""rolag""><dfn>rolag</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolag"" title=""Rolag"">rolag</a> is a loose woolen roll of fibers that results from using handcards.</dd>

<dt id=""roving""><dfn>roving</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roving"" title=""Roving"">roving</a> is a long rope of fibers where all of the fibers have been <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carding"" title=""Carding"">carded</a> to lie parallel to each another.</dd>

<dt id=""rug""><dfn>rug</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpet"" title=""Carpet"">rug</a> is a form of carpet. It is usually smaller than a carpet. See also: <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rug_making"" title=""Rug making"">rug making</a></dd>

</dl>

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""19"" id=""mw1Q"" aria-labelledby=""S""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""S"">S</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19"" title=""Edit section: S""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>
<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt545"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mw1g"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""sailcloth""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Sailcloth]]""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""sateen""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Sateen]] is a fabric formed with a satin weave with weft floats""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""satin""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[satin]] is a cloth that typically has a glossy surface and a dull back. It is formed by a sequence of broken twill floats in either the warp or weft direction, which respectively identify the goods as either a satin or a sateen.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""satin weave""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A satin is a broken twill weaving technique that forms floats on one side of the fabric.  If a satin is woven with the floats parallel to the selvedge of the goods, the corresponding fabric is termed a \""satin.\""  If the floats are perpendicular to the selvedge of the goods, the fabric is termed a &apos;sateen.&apos;\""""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""seam""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[Stitch (textile arts)|seam]], in sewing, is the line where two pieces of fabric are held together by thread.""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""seam ripper""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[seam ripper]] is a small [[tool]] used for unpicking stitches.""}},""i"":12}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""selvage""}},""i"":13}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The [[selvage]] (also &apos;&apos;selvedge&apos;&apos;) is the woven edges of a fabric that lie parallel to the warp.""}},""i"":14}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""serge""}},""i"":15}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Serge (fabric)|Serge]] is a type of twill fabric that has diagonal lines or ridges on both sides, made with a two-up, two-down weave.  The worsted variety is used in making [[military uniforms]], [[suit (clothes)|suit]]s, great and [[trench coat]]s.  Its counterpart, silk serge, is used for linings.  French serge is a softer, finer variety.  The word is also used for a high quality woolen woven.""}},""i"":16}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""serging""}},""i"":17}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Serging]] is the binding off of an edge of cloth.""}},""i"":18}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""sewing""}},""i"":19}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Sewing]] is an ancient craft involving the stitching of cloth, [[leather]], animal skins, [[fur]]s, or other materials, using needle and thread.  Its use is nearly universal among human populations and dates back to [[Paleolithic]] times (30,000 BC).  Sewing predates the weaving of cloth.""}},""i"":20}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""shag""}},""i"":21}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Shag (fabric)]] is typically used to make a deep-pile carpets. This is the oldest use of the term. Shag carpet is sometimes evoked as an example of the [[taste (sociology)#Taste and aesthetics|aesthetic]] from the culture of the [[United States|U.S.]] 1970s. Also used to make carpets for mariners.""}},""i"":22}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""shed""}},""i"":23}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""In weaving, the [[shed (weaving)|shed]] is the gap between warp yarns on a loom when one or more, but not all, of the harnesses are raised.""}},""i"":24}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""sheer""}},""i"":25}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Sheer (textile)|Sheer]] is a semi-transparent and flimsy cloth.""}},""i"":26}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""shoddy""}},""i"":27}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Recycled wool|Recycled or remanufactured wool]]. Historically generated from loosely woven materials. [[Benjamin Law (inventor)|Benjamin Law]] invented shoddy and mungo, as such, in England in 1813. He was the first to organise, on a larger scale, the activity of taking old clothes and grinding them down into a fibrous state that could be re-spun into yarn. The shoddy industry was centred on the towns of [[Batley]], [[Morley, West Yorkshire|Morley]], [[Dewsbury]] and [[Ossett]] in [[West Yorkshire]], and concentrated on the recovery of wool from rags. The importance of the industry can be gauged by the fact that even in 1860 the town of Batley was producing over 7,000 tonnes of shoddy. At the time there were 80 firms employing a total of 550 people sorting the rags. These were then sold to shoddy manufacturers of which there were about 130 in the West Riding.  Shoddy is inferior to the original wool.\n\n\""Shoddy\"" has come to mean \""of poor quality\"" in general (not just clothing), and the original meaning is largely obsolete.""}},""i"":28}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""shot""}},""i"":29}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The opal effect achieved on a fabric by dyeing the warp and weft threads different colours. The yarns are dyed first and then woven. When looking at the fabric from various angles it appears to alter in colour, this is more obvious in lustrous fabrics and more so in certain types of weaves.""}},""i"":30}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""shuttle""}},""i"":31}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[shuttle (weaving)|shuttle]] in weaving is a device used with a loom that is thrown or passed back and forth between the threads of the warp to weave in the weft.""}},""i"":32}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""silk""}},""i"":33}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Silk]] is a natural protein fiber that can be woven into textiles. It is obtained from the [[Cocoon (silk)|cocoon]] of the [[silkworm]] [[larva]], in the process known as &apos;&apos;[[sericulture]]&apos;&apos;, which kills the larvae. The shimmering appearance for which it is prized comes from the fiber&apos;s triangular prism-like structure, which allows silk cloth to refract incoming light at different angles.""}},""i"":34}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""sisal""}},""i"":35}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Sisal]] or sisal hemp is an [[agave]] &apos;&apos;Agave sisalana&apos;&apos; that yields a stiff fiber used in making rope. (The term may refer either to the plant or the fiber, depending on context.) It is not really a variety of hemp, but named so because [[hemp]] was for centuries a major source for fiber, so other fibers were sometimes named after it.""}},""i"":36}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""skein""}},""i"":37}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Hank (textile)|Skein]] is when a length of yarn is bundled in a loose roll rather than put on a cone (as you would purchase from store)- usually done if yarn is going to a dye vat or needs a treatment in a manufacturing/knitting mill environment.""}},""i"":38}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""solution-dyed""}},""i"":39}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Solution-dyed]]""}},""i"":40}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""soufflé""}},""i"":41}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A trade name applied to lightweight textiles, particularly sheer silk (&apos;&apos;soufflé de soie&apos;&apos;) or warp-knitted material used in eveningwear and theatrical costumes. Textile dictionaries also record the name for a loose, soft and spongy novelty fabric made from wool, rayon or silk.{{sfn|Tortora|Johnson|2014|p=576}}""}},""i"":42}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""spandex fiber""}},""i"":43}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Spandex]] or elastane is a synthetic fiber known for its exceptional elasticity (stretchability). It is stronger and more durable than rubber, its major plant competitor. It was [[invention|invented]] in 1959 by [[DuPont]], and when first introduced it revolutionised many areas of the clothing [[Industry (economics)|industry]].""}},""i"":44}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""spinning""}},""i"":45}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Spinning (textiles)|Spinning]] is the process of creating yarn (or thread, rope, cable) from various raw fiber materials.""}},""i"":46}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""spread tow fabric""}},""i"":47}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Spread tow fabric]] is a type of lightweight fabric. Its production involves the steps of spreading a [[tow (fibre)|tow]] of higher count, e.g. 12k, into thin-and-wide [[spread tow tape]] (STT) and weaving them into a lightweight [[fabric]] by employing the novel tape-weaving technique.""}},""i"":48}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""staple""}},""i"":49}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Staple (textiles)|Staple]] is the raw material, or its length and quality, of fiber from which textiles are made.""}},""i"":50}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""stitch""}},""i"":51}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[Stitch (textile arts)|stitch]] is a single turn or loop of the thread or yarn in sewing, knitting and embroidery.{{sfn|Picken|1957|p=322}}""}},""i"":52}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""stuff""}},""i"":53}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Stuff (cloth)|Stuff]] is a coarse cloth, sometimes made with a linen warp and worsted weft.""}},""i"":54}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""super""}},""i"":55}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The super grading system is used to grade the quality of wool fabric. The higher the number, the more yarn is packed in per square inch, therefore all things being equal a super 120s yarn is better than super 100s.""}},""i"":56}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""spun""}},""i"":57}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""See [[Spinning (textiles)|spinning]]""}},""i"":58}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":59}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt545"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt545"" id=""mw1w"">

<dt id=""sailcloth""><dfn>sailcloth</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sailcloth"" title=""Sailcloth"">Sailcloth</a></dd>

<dt id=""sateen""><dfn>sateen</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sateen"" title=""Sateen"">Sateen</a> is a fabric formed with a satin weave with weft floats</dd>

<dt id=""satin""><dfn>satin</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satin"" title=""Satin"">satin</a> is a cloth that typically has a glossy surface and a dull back. It is formed by a sequence of broken twill floats in either the warp or weft direction, which respectively identify the goods as either a satin or a sateen.</dd>

<dt id=""satin_weave""><dfn>satin weave</dfn></dt>
<dd>A satin is a broken twill weaving technique that forms floats on one side of the fabric.  If a satin is woven with the floats parallel to the selvedge of the goods, the corresponding fabric is termed a ""satin.""  If the floats are perpendicular to the selvedge of the goods, the fabric is termed a 'sateen.'""</dd>

<dt id=""seam""><dfn>seam</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stitch_(textile_arts)"" title=""Stitch (textile arts)"">seam</a>, in sewing, is the line where two pieces of fabric are held together by thread.</dd>

<dt id=""seam_ripper""><dfn>seam ripper</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seam_ripper"" title=""Seam ripper"">seam ripper</a> is a small <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tool"" title=""Tool"">tool</a> used for unpicking stitches.</dd>

<dt id=""selvage""><dfn>selvage</dfn></dt>
<dd>The <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selvage"" title=""Selvage"">selvage</a> (also <i>selvedge</i>) is the woven edges of a fabric that lie parallel to the warp.</dd>

<dt id=""serge""><dfn>serge</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serge_(fabric)"" title=""Serge (fabric)"">Serge</a> is a type of twill fabric that has diagonal lines or ridges on both sides, made with a two-up, two-down weave.  The worsted variety is used in making <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_uniforms"" title=""Military uniforms"" class=""mw-redirect"">military uniforms</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suit_(clothes)"" title=""Suit (clothes)"" class=""mw-redirect"">suits</a>, great and <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trench_coat"" title=""Trench coat"">trench coats</a>.  Its counterpart, silk serge, is used for linings.  French serge is a softer, finer variety.  The word is also used for a high quality woolen woven.</dd>

<dt id=""serging""><dfn>serging</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serging"" title=""Serging"" class=""mw-redirect"">Serging</a> is the binding off of an edge of cloth.</dd>

<dt id=""sewing""><dfn>sewing</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sewing"" title=""Sewing"">Sewing</a> is an ancient craft involving the stitching of cloth, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leather"" title=""Leather"">leather</a>, animal skins, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur"" title=""Fur"">furs</a>, or other materials, using needle and thread.  Its use is nearly universal among human populations and dates back to <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic"" title=""Paleolithic"">Paleolithic</a> times (30,000 BC).  Sewing predates the weaving of cloth.</dd>

<dt id=""shag""><dfn>shag</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shag_(fabric)"" title=""Shag (fabric)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Shag (fabric)</a> is typically used to make a deep-pile carpets. This is the oldest use of the term. Shag carpet is sometimes evoked as an example of the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taste_(sociology)#Taste_and_aesthetics"" title=""Taste (sociology)"" class=""mw-redirect"">aesthetic</a> from the culture of the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States"" title=""United States"">U.S.</a> 1970s. Also used to make carpets for mariners.</dd>

<dt id=""shed""><dfn>shed</dfn></dt>
<dd>In weaving, the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shed_(weaving)"" title=""Shed (weaving)"">shed</a> is the gap between warp yarns on a loom when one or more, but not all, of the harnesses are raised.</dd>

<dt id=""sheer""><dfn>sheer</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheer_(textile)"" title=""Sheer (textile)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Sheer</a> is a semi-transparent and flimsy cloth.</dd>

<dt id=""shoddy""><dfn>shoddy</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recycled_wool"" title=""Recycled wool"">Recycled or remanufactured wool</a>. Historically generated from loosely woven materials. <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Law_(inventor)"" title=""Benjamin Law (inventor)"">Benjamin Law</a> invented shoddy and mungo, as such, in England in 1813. He was the first to organise, on a larger scale, the activity of taking old clothes and grinding them down into a fibrous state that could be re-spun into yarn. The shoddy industry was centred on the towns of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batley"" title=""Batley"">Batley</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morley,_West_Yorkshire"" title=""Morley, West Yorkshire"">Morley</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dewsbury"" title=""Dewsbury"">Dewsbury</a> and <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ossett"" title=""Ossett"">Ossett</a> in <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Yorkshire"" title=""West Yorkshire"">West Yorkshire</a>, and concentrated on the recovery of wool from rags. The importance of the industry can be gauged by the fact that even in 1860 the town of Batley was producing over 7,000 tonnes of shoddy. At the time there were 80 firms employing a total of 550 people sorting the rags. These were then sold to shoddy manufacturers of which there were about 130 in the West Riding.  Shoddy is inferior to the original wool.

""Shoddy"" has come to mean ""of poor quality"" in general (not just clothing), and the original meaning is largely obsolete.</dd>

<dt id=""shot""><dfn>shot</dfn></dt>
<dd>The opal effect achieved on a fabric by dyeing the warp and weft threads different colours. The yarns are dyed first and then woven. When looking at the fabric from various angles it appears to alter in colour, this is more obvious in lustrous fabrics and more so in certain types of weaves.</dd>

<dt id=""shuttle""><dfn>shuttle</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shuttle_(weaving)"" title=""Shuttle (weaving)"">shuttle</a> in weaving is a device used with a loom that is thrown or passed back and forth between the threads of the warp to weave in the weft.</dd>

<dt id=""silk""><dfn>silk</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk"" title=""Silk"">Silk</a> is a natural protein fiber that can be woven into textiles. It is obtained from the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoon_(silk)"" title=""Cocoon (silk)"" class=""mw-redirect"">cocoon</a> of the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silkworm"" title=""Silkworm"" class=""mw-redirect"">silkworm</a> <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larva"" title=""Larva"">larva</a>, in the process known as <i><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sericulture"" title=""Sericulture"">sericulture</a></i>, which kills the larvae. The shimmering appearance for which it is prized comes from the fiber's triangular prism-like structure, which allows silk cloth to refract incoming light at different angles.</dd>

<dt id=""sisal""><dfn>sisal</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisal"" title=""Sisal"">Sisal</a> or sisal hemp is an <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agave"" title=""Agave"">agave</a> <i>Agave sisalana</i> that yields a stiff fiber used in making rope. (The term may refer either to the plant or the fiber, depending on context.) It is not really a variety of hemp, but named so because <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemp"" title=""Hemp"">hemp</a> was for centuries a major source for fiber, so other fibers were sometimes named after it.</dd>

<dt id=""skein""><dfn>skein</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hank_(textile)"" title=""Hank (textile)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Skein</a> is when a length of yarn is bundled in a loose roll rather than put on a cone (as you would purchase from store)- usually done if yarn is going to a dye vat or needs a treatment in a manufacturing/knitting mill environment.</dd>

<dt id=""solution-dyed""><dfn>solution-dyed</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solution-dyed?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Solution-dyed (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Solution-dyed""]}}'>Solution-dyed</a></dd>

<dt id=""soufflé""><dfn>soufflé</dfn></dt>
<dd>A trade name applied to lightweight textiles, particularly sheer silk (<i>soufflé de soie</i>) or warp-knitted material used in eveningwear and theatrical costumes. Textile dictionaries also record the name for a loose, soft and spongy novelty fabric made from wool, rayon or silk.<sup about=""#mwt589"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-FOOTNOTETortoraJohnson2014576_26-0"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{""group"":"""",""name"":""FOOTNOTETortoraJohnson2014576""},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTETortoraJohnson2014576-26""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-FOOTNOTETortoraJohnson2014576-26"" id=""mw2A""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mw2Q""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mw2g"">[</span>26<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mw2w"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""spandex_fiber""><dfn>spandex fiber</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spandex"" title=""Spandex"">Spandex</a> or elastane is a synthetic fiber known for its exceptional elasticity (stretchability). It is stronger and more durable than rubber, its major plant competitor. It was <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invention"" title=""Invention"">invented</a> in 1959 by <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DuPont"" title=""DuPont"">DuPont</a>, and when first introduced it revolutionised many areas of the clothing <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_(economics)"" title=""Industry (economics)"">industry</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""spinning""><dfn>spinning</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinning_(textiles)"" title=""Spinning (textiles)"">Spinning</a> is the process of creating yarn (or thread, rope, cable) from various raw fiber materials.</dd>

<dt id=""spread_tow_fabric""><dfn>spread tow fabric</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_tow_fabric"" title=""Spread tow fabric"">Spread tow fabric</a> is a type of lightweight fabric. Its production involves the steps of spreading a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tow_(fibre)"" title=""Tow (fibre)"">tow</a> of higher count, e.g. 12k, into thin-and-wide <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_tow_tape"" title=""Spread tow tape"" class=""mw-redirect"">spread tow tape</a> (STT) and weaving them into a lightweight <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabric"" title=""Fabric"" class=""mw-redirect"">fabric</a> by employing the novel tape-weaving technique.</dd>

<dt id=""staple""><dfn>staple</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staple_(textiles)"" title=""Staple (textiles)"">Staple</a> is the raw material, or its length and quality, of fiber from which textiles are made.</dd>

<dt id=""stitch""><dfn>stitch</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stitch_(textile_arts)"" title=""Stitch (textile arts)"">stitch</a> is a single turn or loop of the thread or yarn in sewing, knitting and embroidery.<sup about=""#mwt601"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPicken1957322_27-0"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{""group"":"""",""name"":""FOOTNOTEPicken1957322""},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEPicken1957322-27""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPicken1957322-27"" id=""mw3A""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mw3Q""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mw3g"">[</span>27<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mw3w"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""stuff""><dfn>stuff</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuff_(cloth)"" title=""Stuff (cloth)"">Stuff</a> is a coarse cloth, sometimes made with a linen warp and worsted weft.</dd>

<dt id=""super""><dfn>super</dfn></dt>
<dd>The super grading system is used to grade the quality of wool fabric. The higher the number, the more yarn is packed in per square inch, therefore all things being equal a super 120s yarn is better than super 100s.</dd>

<dt id=""spun""><dfn>spun</dfn></dt>
<dd>See <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinning_(textiles)"" title=""Spinning (textiles)"">spinning</a></dd>

</dl>

<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt614"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mw5A"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""tablet weaving""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Tablet weaving]] is a process of weaving where tablets, also called &apos;cards&apos;, are used to create the shed that the weft is passed through. It is generally used to make [[narrow work]] such as belts or straps.""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Tactel""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Tactel (fiber)|Tactel]] is the [[brand]] name of a man-made fiber made from nylon.""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""taffeta""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Taffeta]] is a type of fabric, often used for fancy dresses.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""tapestry""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Tapestry]] is a form of textile art.  It is woven by hand on a weaving-loom. The chain thread is the carrier in which the coloured striking thread is woven.  In this way, a colourful pattern or image is created. Most weavers use a naturally based chain thread made out of linen or wool.  The striking threads can be made out of silk, wool, gold or silver, but can also be made out of any form of textile.""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""tarlatan""}},""i"":9}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Tarlatan (also &apos;&apos;&apos;tarlaton&apos;&apos;&apos;) is a [[starch]]ed, open-weave fabric, much like [[cheesecloth]]. In [[printmaking]], it can be used to wipe the [[ink]] off a plate during the [[intaglio (printmaking)|intaglio]] inking process.  The open weave allows for the tarlatan to pick up a large quantity of ink.  The stiffness imparted by the starch helps prevent the fabric from taking the ink out of the incised lines.""}},""i"":10}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""tassel""}},""i"":11}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[tassel]] is a ball-shaped bunch of plaited or otherwise entangled threads from which at one end protrudes a [[cord (sewing)|cord]] on which the tassel is hung, and which may have loose, dangling threads at the other end.""}},""i"":12}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""tatting""}},""i"":13}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Tatting]] is a technique for handcrafting lace that can be documented approximately to the early 19th century.""}},""i"":14}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""terry cloth""}},""i"":15}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Terrycloth|Terry cloth]] is a type of cloth with loops sticking out. Most [[bath towels]] are examples of terry cloth.""}},""i"":16}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""thimble""}},""i"":17}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""A [[thimble]] is a protective shield worn on the finger or thumb.""}},""i"":18}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""threads per inch (TPI)""}},""i"":19}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Thread count|Threads per inch]] is the measurement of the number of threads per inch of material, such as fabric, or metal in [[threads per inch|the case of screws and bolts]].""}},""i"":20}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""thread count""}},""i"":21}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""The [[Thread count#Thread count|thread count]] is the number of warp threads per inch plus the number of weft threads.""}},""i"":22}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""tissue""}},""i"":23}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""Tissue is a fine woven fabric or gauze.""}},""i"":24}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""trim""}},""i"":25}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Trim (sewing)|Trim]] or &apos;&apos;&apos;trimming&apos;&apos;&apos; in clothing and [[home decorating]] is applied ornament such as [[Gimp (thread)|gimp]], [[passementerie]], [[ribbon]], [[Ruffle (sewing)|ruffles]], or, as a [[verb]], to apply such ornament.""}},""i"":26}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""tulle""}},""i"":27}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Tulle netting|Tulle]] is a netting, which is often starched, made of various fibers, including silk, nylon, and rayon, that is most commonly used for [[veil]]s, [[gown]]s (particularly wedding gowns) and [[ballet tutu]]s.""}},""i"":28}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""tweed""}},""i"":29}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Tweed (cloth)|Tweed]] is a type of fabric using the twill weave.""}},""i"":30}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""twill tape""}},""i"":31}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Twill tape]] is a flat twill-woven ribbon of cotton, linen, polyester, or wool.""}},""i"":32}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""twill weave""}},""i"":33}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Twill]] is a type of fabric woven with a pattern of diagonal parallel ribs. It is made by passing the weft threads over one warp thread and then under two or more warp threads. Examples of twill fabric are gabardine, tweed and serge.""}},""i"":34}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":35}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt614"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt614"" id=""mw5Q"">

<dt id=""tablet_weaving""><dfn>tablet weaving</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tablet_weaving"" title=""Tablet weaving"">Tablet weaving</a> is a process of weaving where tablets, also called 'cards', are used to create the shed that the weft is passed through. It is generally used to make <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrow_work?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Narrow work (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Narrow work""]}}'>narrow work</a> such as belts or straps.</dd>

<dt id=""tactel""><dfn>Tactel</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tactel_(fiber)?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Tactel (fiber) (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Tactel (fiber)""]}}'>Tactel</a> is the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brand"" title=""Brand"">brand</a> name of a man-made fiber made from nylon.</dd>

<dt id=""taffeta""><dfn>taffeta</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taffeta"" title=""Taffeta"">Taffeta</a> is a type of fabric, often used for fancy dresses.</dd>

<dt id=""tapestry""><dfn>tapestry</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapestry"" title=""Tapestry"">Tapestry</a> is a form of textile art.  It is woven by hand on a weaving-loom. The chain thread is the carrier in which the coloured striking thread is woven.  In this way, a colourful pattern or image is created. Most weavers use a naturally based chain thread made out of linen or wool.  The striking threads can be made out of silk, wool, gold or silver, but can also be made out of any form of textile.</dd>

<dt id=""tarlatan""><dfn>tarlatan</dfn></dt>
<dd>Tarlatan (also <b>tarlaton</b>) is a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starch"" title=""Starch"">starched</a>, open-weave fabric, much like <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheesecloth"" title=""Cheesecloth"">cheesecloth</a>. In <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Printmaking"" title=""Printmaking"">printmaking</a>, it can be used to wipe the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ink"" title=""Ink"">ink</a> off a plate during the <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intaglio_(printmaking)"" title=""Intaglio (printmaking)"">intaglio</a> inking process.  The open weave allows for the tarlatan to pick up a large quantity of ink.  The stiffness imparted by the starch helps prevent the fabric from taking the ink out of the incised lines.</dd>

<dt id=""tassel""><dfn>tassel</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tassel"" title=""Tassel"">tassel</a> is a ball-shaped bunch of plaited or otherwise entangled threads from which at one end protrudes a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cord_(sewing)"" title=""Cord (sewing)"">cord</a> on which the tassel is hung, and which may have loose, dangling threads at the other end.</dd>

<dt id=""tatting""><dfn>tatting</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatting"" title=""Tatting"">Tatting</a> is a technique for handcrafting lace that can be documented approximately to the early 19th century.</dd>

<dt id=""terry_cloth""><dfn>terry cloth</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrycloth"" title=""Terrycloth"">Terry cloth</a> is a type of cloth with loops sticking out. Most <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath_towels"" title=""Bath towels"" class=""mw-redirect"">bath towels</a> are examples of terry cloth.</dd>

<dt id=""thimble""><dfn>thimble</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thimble"" title=""Thimble"">thimble</a> is a protective shield worn on the finger or thumb.</dd>

<dt id=""threads_per_inch_(tpi)""><dfn>threads per inch (TPI)</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thread_count"" title=""Thread count"" class=""mw-redirect"">Threads per inch</a> is the measurement of the number of threads per inch of material, such as fabric, or metal in <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Threads_per_inch"" title=""Threads per inch"" class=""mw-redirect"">the case of screws and bolts</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""thread_count""><dfn>thread count</dfn></dt>
<dd>The <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thread_count#Thread_count"" title=""Thread count"" class=""mw-redirect"">thread count</a> is the number of warp threads per inch plus the number of weft threads.</dd>

<dt id=""tissue""><dfn>tissue</dfn></dt>
<dd>Tissue is a fine woven fabric or gauze.</dd>

<dt id=""trim""><dfn>trim</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trim_(sewing)"" title=""Trim (sewing)"">Trim</a> or <b>trimming</b> in clothing and <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_decorating"" title=""Home decorating"" class=""mw-redirect"">home decorating</a> is applied ornament such as <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimp_(thread)"" title=""Gimp (thread)"">gimp</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passementerie"" title=""Passementerie"">passementerie</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribbon"" title=""Ribbon"">ribbon</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruffle_(sewing)"" title=""Ruffle (sewing)"">ruffles</a>, or, as a <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verb"" title=""Verb"">verb</a>, to apply such ornament.</dd>

<dt id=""tulle""><dfn>tulle</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulle_netting"" title=""Tulle netting"" class=""mw-redirect"">Tulle</a> is a netting, which is often starched, made of various fibers, including silk, nylon, and rayon, that is most commonly used for <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veil"" title=""Veil"">veils</a>, <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gown"" title=""Gown"">gowns</a> (particularly wedding gowns) and <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballet_tutu"" title=""Ballet tutu"" class=""mw-redirect"">ballet tutus</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""tweed""><dfn>tweed</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tweed_(cloth)"" title=""Tweed (cloth)"" class=""mw-redirect"">Tweed</a> is a type of fabric using the twill weave.</dd>

<dt id=""twill_tape""><dfn>twill tape</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twill_tape"" title=""Twill tape"">Twill tape</a> is a flat twill-woven ribbon of cotton, linen, polyester, or wool.</dd>

<dt id=""twill_weave""><dfn>twill weave</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twill"" title=""Twill"">Twill</a> is a type of fabric woven with a pattern of diagonal parallel ribs. It is made by passing the weft threads over one warp thread and then under two or more warp threads. Examples of twill fabric are gabardine, tweed and serge.</dd>

</dl>

</section><section data-mw-section-id=""22"" id=""mw7g"" aria-labelledby=""V""><div class=""mw-heading mw-heading2""><h2 id=""V"">V</h2><span class=""mw-editsection""><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">[</span><a href=""/w/index.php?title=Glossary_of_textile_manufacturing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22"" title=""Edit section: V""><span>edit</span></a><span class=""mw-editsection-bracket"">]</span></span></div>

<link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1228772891"" about=""#mwt660"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles mw:Transclusion"" id=""mw8A"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Glossary/styles.css""},""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""glossary"",""href"":""./Template:Glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":0}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""velour""}},""i"":1}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Velour]] is a textile, a knitted counterpart of velvet.\nIt combines the stretchy properties of knits such as spandex with the rich appearance and feel of velvet.""}},""i"":2}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""velvet""}},""i"":3}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Velvet]] is a type of [[tufted]] fabric in which the cut threads are very evenly distributed, with a short dense pile, giving it its distinct feel.  Velvet can be made from any fiber. It is woven on a special loom that weaves two piece of velvet at the same time.   The two pieces are then cut apart and the two lengths of fabric are wound on separate take-up rolls.""}},""i"":4}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""velveteen""}},""i"":5}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Velveteen]] is a cotton cloth made in imitation of velvet. The term is sometimes applied to a mixture of silk and cotton. Some velveteens are a kind of fustian, having a rib of velvet pile alternating with a plain depression. The velveteen, trade varies a good deal with the fashions that control the production of velvet.""}},""i"":6}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""term"",""href"":""./Template:Term""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""viscose""}},""i"":7}},""\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""defn"",""href"":""./Template:Defn""},""params"":{""1"":{""wt"":""[[Viscose]] is an artificial cellulose-based polymer, sometimes used as a synonym for [[#R|Rayon]].""}},""i"":8}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":9}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt660"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt660"" id=""mw8Q"">

<dt id=""velour""><dfn>velour</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velour"" title=""Velour"">Velour</a> is a textile, a knitted counterpart of velvet.
It combines the stretchy properties of knits such as spandex with the rich appearance and feel of velvet.</dd>

<dt id=""velvet""><dfn>velvet</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velvet"" title=""Velvet"">Velvet</a> is a type of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tufted"" title=""Tufted"" class=""mw-redirect"">tufted</a> fabric in which the cut threads are very evenly distributed, with a short dense pile, giving it its distinct feel.  Velvet can be made from any fiber. It is woven on a special loom that weaves two piece of velvet at the same time.   The two pieces are then cut apart and the two lengths of fabric are wound on separate take-up rolls.</dd>

<dt id=""velveteen""><dfn>velveteen</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velveteen"" title=""Velveteen"">Velveteen</a> is a cotton cloth made in imitation of velvet. The term is sometimes applied to a mixture of silk and cotton. Some velveteens are a kind of fustian, having a rib of velvet pile alternating with a plain depression. The velveteen, trade varies a good deal with the fashions that control the production of velvet.</dd>

<dt id=""viscose""><dfn>viscose</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viscose"" title=""Viscose"" class=""mw-redirect"">Viscose</a> is an artificial cellulose-based polymer, sometimes used as a synonym for <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#R"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment"">Rayon</a>.</dd>

</dl>
<span class=""mw-empty-elt"" about=""#mwt671"" typeof=""mw:Transclusion"" id=""mw8g"" data-mw='{""parts"":[{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""compact ToC"",""href"":""./Template:Compact_ToC""},""params"":{""name"":{""wt"":""no""},""nobreak"":{""wt"":""yes""},""top"":{""wt"":""yes""},""extlinks"":{""wt"":""yes""}},""i"":0}}]}'><meta property=""mw:PageProp/notoc""/></span><div class=""noprint "" about=""#mwt671"" id=""mw8w""><div role=""navigation"" id=""toc"" class=""toc plainlinks"" aria-label=""Contents"" style="" text-align:left;""><link rel=""mw-deduplicated-inline-style"" href=""mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1374427404"" about=""#mwt672"" typeof=""mw:Extension/templatestyles"" data-mw='{""name"":""templatestyles"",""attrs"":{""src"":""Hlist/styles.css""}}'/><div class=""hlist "">

It only stretches in the bias directions (between the warp and weft directions), unless the threads are elastic. Woven cloth usually [[wikt:unravel|fray]]s at the edges, unless measures are taken to counter this, such as the use of [[pinking shears]] or hemming.""}},""i"":28}},""\n\n"",{""template"":{""target"":{""wt"":""end glossary"",""href"":""./Template:End_glossary""},""params"":{},""i"":29}}]}'/><span about=""#mwt675"">
</span><dl class=""glossary "" about=""#mwt675"" id=""mw9w"">

<dt id=""warp""><dfn>warp</dfn></dt>
<dd>The <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warp_(weaving)"" title=""Warp (weaving)"" class=""mw-redirect"">warp</a> is the set of lengthwise threads attached to a loom before weaving begins, and through which the weft is woven. The warp threads run vertically.<sup about=""#mwt681"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-28"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-28""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-28"" id=""mw-A""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mw-Q""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mw-g"">[</span>28<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mw-w"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""warp_knit""><dfn>warp knit</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warp_knitting"" title=""Warp knitting"">Warp knit</a></dd>

<dt id=""waterproof""><dfn>waterproof</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterproof"" title=""Waterproof"" class=""mw-redirect"">Waterproof</a></dd>

<dt id=""water_repellent""><dfn>water repellent</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_repellent"" title=""Water repellent"" class=""mw-redirect"">Water repellent</a></dd>

<dt id=""weaving""><dfn>weaving</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaving"" title=""Weaving"">Weaving</a> is an ancient textile art and craft that involves placing two sets of threads or yarn made of fiber called the warp and weft of the loom and turning them into cloth. This cloth can be plain (in one color or a simple pattern), or it can be woven in decorative or artistic designs, including tapestries.</dd>

<dt id=""weft""><dfn>weft</dfn></dt>
<dd>The <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weft"" title=""Weft"" class=""mw-redirect"">weft</a> is the yarn that is woven back and forth through the warp to make cloth. This set of yarn or thread is also known as the filling.<sup about=""#mwt696"" class=""mw-ref reference"" id=""cite_ref-29"" rel=""dc:references"" typeof=""mw:Extension/ref"" data-mw='{""name"":""ref"",""attrs"":{},""body"":{""id"":""mw-reference-text-cite_note-29""}}'><a href=""#cite_note-29"" id=""mw_A""><span class=""mw-reflink-text"" id=""mw_Q""><span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mw_g"">[</span>29<span class=""cite-bracket"" id=""mw_w"">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>

<dt id=""weft_knit""><dfn>weft knit</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weft_knit"" title=""Weft knit"" class=""mw-redirect"">Weft knit</a></dd>

<dt id=""wilton_carpet""><dfn>Wilton Carpet</dfn></dt>
<dd>Wilton carpet is produced on a specific type of weaving machine called wire loom.  Wilton carpets are <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pile_carpet"" title=""Pile carpet"" class=""mw-redirect"">pile carpets</a> whereby the pile is formed by inserting steel rods in the pile warps of the fabric. After extraction of the rods the pile is looped (in case straight wires have been used) or cut (in case cutting wires are used).  Wilton carpet is generally considered as high quality and is used for heavy duty applications. It is named after <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilton,_Wiltshire"" title=""Wilton, Wiltshire"">Wilton, Wiltshire</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""wire_loom""><dfn>wire loom</dfn></dt>
<dd>Weaving machine for pile fabrics or velvets whereby the pile is made by weaving steel rods or wires into the fabrics.  When the wires are extracted the warp ends that have been woven over the wires remain as loops on top of the fabric or will form cut pile if the wire is equipped with a cutting blade.  This technique is also known as ""épinglé weaving"". A wire loom in a much wider version (up to 5 meters of width) and in heavier construction is used for the manufacturing of carpets is called a ""WILTON"" loom, and the carpets made on such a loom are known as ""<a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilton_Carpet?action=edit&amp;redlink=1"" title=""Wilton Carpet (page does not exist)"" class=""new"" typeof=""mw:LocalizedAttrs"" data-mw-i18n='{""title"":{""lang"":""x-page"",""key"":""red-link-title"",""params"":[""Wilton Carpet""]}}'>Wilton Carpets</a>"".</dd>

<dt id=""woof""><dfn>woof</dfn></dt>
<dd>Another term for <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""#weft"" class=""mw-selflink-fragment""><span title=""See entry on this page at § weft"" class=""glossary-link-internal "" style=""border-bottom:1px dashed #86a1ff;color:var(--color-base,#202122);"">weft</span></a>.</dd>

<dt id=""wool""><dfn>wool</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wool"" title=""Wool"">Wool</a> is the fiber derived from the hair of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domesticated_animal"" title=""Domesticated animal"" class=""mw-redirect"">domesticated animals</a>, usually <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheep"" title=""Sheep"">sheep</a>.</dd>

<dt id=""woolen""><dfn>woolen</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woolen"" title=""Woolen"">Woolen</a> or <b>woollen</b> is a yarn and cloth usually made from wool.</dd>

<dt id=""worsted_fabric""><dfn>worsted fabric</dfn></dt>
<dd><a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worsted"" title=""Worsted"">Worsted</a> is a yarn and cloth usually made from wool. The yarn is well twisted and spun of long staple wool (though nowadays also medium and short fibers are used). The wool is combed so that the fibers lie parallel.</dd>

<dt id=""woven_fabric""><dfn>woven fabric</dfn></dt>
<dd>A <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woven"" title=""Woven"" class=""mw-redirect"">woven</a> fabric is a cloth formed by weaving. It only stretches in the bias directions (between the warp and weft directions), unless the threads are elastic. Woven cloth usually <a rel=""mw:WikiLink/Interwiki"" href=""https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/unravel"" title=""wikt:unravel"" class=""extiw"">frays</a> at the edges, unless measures are taken to counter this, such as the use of <a rel=""mw:WikiLink"" href=""https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinking_shears"" title=""Pinking shears"">pinking shears</a> or hemming.</dd>

</dl>
